We introduce DIB-Bench, a benchmark that measures this deployment-integrity blindness across 7,560 scored examples, six tamper types, and an evaluator ladder from regex scorers through LLM judges to GPT-5-nano. Stronger evaluators accept more tampered outputs: mean acceptance rises from 88.6% (rule-based) to 94.1% (GPT-5-nano), and a trigger-conditional tamper invisible on the evaluation suite produces 48-100% harmful outputs, rising with model capability. A PAC-style bound shows that tampers with zero output shift are undetectable from outputs at any sample size; a manifest-hash identity channel detects them with zero samples. Behavioral fingerprinting identifies the model component above chance (76.7%) but covers one of six stack components. We also propose a graded deployment-identity descriptor for evaluation reports.

This paper challenges the understanding capabilities of existing AI paradigms and emerging world models by asking a fundamental question: How quickly can reality change before an intelligent system can no longer reliably perceive, reason, and act? We formulate this as a theory- and model-agnostic challenge in which systems must infer reliable actions for increasingly distant future states across a growing observation--reality gap. We operationalize this challenge through the effective Speed of Coherent Cognition (\(S_C^{\mathrm{eff}}\)), defined as the highest tested Speed of Reality (\(S_R\)) through which a system continuously satisfies a predefined behavioural reliability criterion from baseline. As a proof of concept, reinforcement-learning agents are trained at the baseline \(S_R\) and evaluated, without retraining, as \(S_R\) is increased by up to \(50\times\) while computational resources remain fixed. We compare Transformer and gated-MLP baselines with \(Co^4\), an architecture inspired by cellular mechanisms implicated in conscious processing and awake thought. Across the tested scenarios, \(Co^4\) achieves substantially higher \(S_C^{\mathrm{eff}}\) than the baselines, motivating \(S_C^{\mathrm{eff}}\) as a new benchmark and the scaling of coherent cognition as a principle for reliable AI.

Real-time interaction requires agents to perceive evolving environments and act under temporal constraints, yet existing evaluations of real-time streaming models focus on conversational quality and streaming perception, leaving effective action under strict temporal constraints untested. We introduce Realtime-Bench, a suite of carefully curated dynamic tasks where delayed actions directly affect success. Under Wall Clock, where environments advance according to actual inference latency, the best success rate reaches only 44% and the best Pass^3 is only 36%, demonstrating that real-time interaction remains challenging. To separate serving latency from models' real-time streaming capability, we compare Wall Clock with Paused Clock, which removes inference delays as an idealized reference, and introduce Token Clock, which advances simulated time according to generated token counts, testing temporal awareness independently of physical serving speed. We find that temporal costs limit success in evolving environments and that higher reasoning effort has mixed effects under time pressure, reflecting a trade-off between decision quality and timely execution. These findings suggest directions for improving real-time multimodal agents, including adaptive reasoning with temporal awareness and continuous perception and action.

Few-shot adaptation often relies on an encoder's final global representation, leaving complementary information in intermediate layers underused. A single forward pass per view supplies all descriptors. Global and support-calibrated regional kernel predictors independently fit the same label residuals. Labeled validation data select their mixture in class-score space, preserving native feature dimensions without learned projections. LAMP-F further adapts text-anchored class prototypes, using a cached response to update the fixed-kernel residual predictions exactly. Experiments cover eleven datasets and ViT and ResNet backbones. LAMP-F reaches 16-shot accuracies of 83.57% and 79.10%, respectively. Controlled experiments identify multi-layer integration as the main source of improvement and examine pooling, calibration, validation labels, and computational cost. These findings show that combining layer-specific predictions can exploit complementary features from frozen encoders to improve few-shot classification.

[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21750) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.21750) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.21750) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.21750)

2026/10/16 09:22 GTM

Artificial intelligence systems deployed in high-risk settings increasingly require models that support meaningful human oversight. Concept-based models (CMs) offer a promising framework by enabling users to inspect and intervene on intermediate, human-understandable concepts. However, concept interventions can counterintuitively worsen downstream predictions, a failure mode we formalise as intervention harm. We distinguish harm after all concepts have been intervened on from harm under partial interventions, the more likely practical use case. Modern CMs often achieve high accuracy by encoding, or leaking, additional task-relevant information in their intermediate concept representations. Recent work argues that such leakage can be benign with respect to intervention harm when concept representations satisfy two conditions: sufficiency and localisation. We prove that these criteria are not sufficient to guarantee that concept interventions improve downstream performance. Across controlled and real-world settings, we find that concept supervision incompleteness is associated with increased intervention harm. Importantly, optimising the model when all concepts are intervened on, a previously proposed leakage mitigation objective, reduces endpoint harm but does not reliably prevent harmful partial corrections. Hence, we introduce a complementary monotonic intervention objective that directly penalises increases in task loss along partial intervention trajectories. Combining endpoint and monotonic intervention training substantially reduces the prevalence and magnitude of partial intervention harm while preserving concept and task predictive performance.

Understanding a conversation requires connecting what is said to the speakers' relationships, expectations, and earlier exchanges. This motivates training models to discover the cues that matter and use them to support an interpretation. We propose CLUE, a two-stage training pipeline that combines supervised evidence reasoning with GRPO guided by Grounded Information Utilization ($\mathrm{IU}_{\mathrm{G}}$). This reward credits new, evidence-supported social inferences while accounting for reasoning effort. At inference time, the trained model reasons over the supplied interaction in a self-contained manner. Compared with base Qwen, CLUE achieves relative gains of 17.3% in-domain and 9.3% out-of-domain in average weighted F1, with higher F1 on four of five out-of-domain evaluations. These gains are accompanied by over 80% fewer reasoning tokens on average in both domains. Human evaluation further finds that CLUE more often grounds its explanations in specific conversational content, with closer alignment between its quoted evidence and human-selected context.

Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) enhances the capabilities of large language models (LLMs) by querying external structured knowledge. However, it can also introduce privacy risks by leaking sensitive information from the retrieval datastore. By applying the same projection to both datastore embeddings and query embeddings, our method provably preserves semantic similarity between queries and retrieved items while substantially mitigating data extraction attacks. Across multiple RAG architectures and datasets, we show that this lightweight approach achieves superior retrieval and generation performance compared to prior methods which leverage formal differential privacy (DP) guarantees, while exhibiting comparable empirical privacy under strong attack models. Our results for the first time suggest that random projection can serve as a competitive and practical baseline for privacy-preserving RAG systems.

[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22150) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.22150) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.22150) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.22150)

2026/10/16 09:22 GTM

In Rhetorical Structure Theory (RST), each edge of a discourse tree carries a nuclearity label, marking which span holds the central content, and a relation drawn from a closed inventory of functional categories. Neither label is a surface property: the same two clauses can be Elaboration or Background depending on rhetorical intent, and the boundary between them is fixed by annotation convention rather than by lexical cues. Predicting these labels therefore requires a formal discourse schema, and exposure to discourse-rich pretraining text does not supply it: given only a target EDU pair, a strong LLM recovers nuclearity at 0.794 F1 and relation at 0.738 F1. We propose RAG-RST, a retrieval-augmented framework that supplies this schema as in-context demonstration rather than through parameter updates, raising nuclearity to 0.846 F1 and relation to 0.788 F1 with no training. Two configurations are compared: a precomputed strategy that labels all EDU pairs before parsing, and a dynamic strategy that retrieves at each reduce step, so that the exemplars reaching the generator depend on the reductions the parser has already committed to. Both outperform existing RST parsers across span, nuclearity, relation, and full tree metrics, with the dynamic variant consistently strongest. Two properties of the retrieved schema emerge: its utility is bounded in number, saturating and then declining beyond fifty exemplars, and it depends on structural position. Both indicate that a formal linguistic schema can be supplied to a foundation model as a demonstration rather than trained into its parameters.

However, most agentic benchmarks have focused more on automation. Strong performance on such benchmarks does not imply effective real-world collaboration. Professional workflows offer a useful setting, as they often involve interdependent steps. Kolab also contains participants' collaboration and output quality judgments. Our analysis reveals diverse collaboration patterns, highlighting the importance of when and how professionals coordinate with agents. A collaborative agent must do two things that automation benchmarks never test: anticipate whether experts will adopt, modify, or reject its contribution once it leaves the chat window to guide its next steps, and assess collaboration quality from the unfolding workflow without constant expert feedback. Our benchmark operationalizes both: (1) forecasting how professionals use agent contributions at diverse workflows, and (2) verifying their judgments of collaboration satisfaction and output quality. Findings show that frontier models, both agentic and non-agentic, overestimate collaboration with experts and struggle to distinguish between high- and low-quality collaboration. Longer workflow context does not consistently improve these predictions. As frontier agents cannot yet understand long-horizon collaboration with experts, we position Kolab as a valuable dataset for studying human-agent collaboration grounded in real professional workflows.

Spiking neural networks (SNNs) offer a compelling pathway toward energy-efficient artificial intelligence, fundamentally anchored in presynaptic input-driven sparsity. By incorporating a state-driven dynamic gate, the DSN allows a neuron to enter a dormant state when its membrane potential falls below a predefined negative threshold, dynamically pruning redundant computations at the instance level. Crucially, this dormancy mechanism is directly integrated into the neuronal dynamics and trained end-to-end via backpropagation through time using surrogate gradients, enabling the network to actively learn a robust dormancy policy that adaptively preserves critical information flow. Extensive experiments across static and neuromorphic datasets demonstrate that the DSN framework consistently reduces computational energy by 40% to over 70%. Notably, on the large-scale ImageNet dataset, the DSN outperforms state-of-the-art static pruning techniques at comparable reported cost reductions and simultaneously improves the corresponding baseline SNN accuracy by 1.34%. By establishing a trainable, state-driven sparsity paradigm orthogonal to conventional input-driven methods, the DSN unlocks synergistic efficiency gains, presenting an effective approach for advancing neuromorphic computing.

In LLM-based multi-agent systems (MAS), a local mistake can propagate as other agents build on it, turning a correctable error into a failed task. We present Agent Runtime Inspection and Amendment (ARIA), a framework for learning when and how to repair ongoing multi-agent executions. Our key intuition is to turn execution-verified repairs into reusable supervision, moving repair search into data construction and training so that a learned model can intervene at runtime without repeatedly testing alternatives. First, we collect over 24{,}000 successful and failed trajectories across four multi-agent frameworks, six benchmarks, and eight host models. From these trajectories, we construct the ARIA dataset, to our knowledge the first large-scale dataset pairing failed multi-agent trajectories with execution-verified repairs. It contains 3{,}953 verified repair pairs, each specifying what went wrong, where to intervene, and how execution should continue. Then, we develop the ARIA framework for MAS-agnostic runtime inspection and amendment, designed to integrate with any MAS that exposes execution trajectories and supports continuation replacement. Using verified repairs as training examples, we teach a compact model to recognize when intervention is needed and how to correct an ongoing run. The aim is to learn repair strategies that apply to related failures in new configurations, rather than merely reproduce individual corrections. Finally, the trained ARIA model demonstrates strong repair performance in both post-hoc and runtime settings, with generalization to unseen frameworks and host models. An ensemble of language models is a jury, not an electorate. The answers are testimony from witnesses of unknown reliability, and the question is not what the majority said but who is credible. Most ensemble methods assign each expert a fixed reputation, often learned from labels. We ask whether it can instead be inferred from the pattern of agreement and disagreement among the experts. An incorrect solution may nevertheless contain useful intermediate reasoning, so its value as supervision need not be determined solely by its final answer. Can a model improve by learning from its unverified peer solutions without first determining which are correct? 2026/10/15 11:39 GTM

Federated LLM unlearning addresses targeted knowledge removal when forget and retain data remain distributed across private clients and are inaccessible to the server. The resulting locally trained adapters encode conflicting objectives, making their aggregation fundamentally different from conventional federated optimization. A key failure mode is magnitude explosion, whereby unnormalized cross-client aggregation increases the merged update magnitude and degrades retained utility under stronger unlearning. We propose FIRM (Fisher-Informed Retention Merging), a single-round framework for stable and retention-aware server-side aggregation. FIRM uses client-local Fisher statistics as a unified sensitivity signal to construct a Fisher-normalized base merge and impose explicit control over retain-sensitive parameters. Its second-order retention projection admits a unique minimum-distance solution, while the corresponding dual scales with the number of retain clients rather than the model dimension. Experiments across WMDP, TOFU, and MUSE-HP using Zephyr-7B and Llama-3-8B show utility gains at comparable forgetting on WMDP and MUSE-HP, with metric-dependent trade-offs on TOFU. Controlled comparisons support benefits of the combined base merge and additional retention correction at selected operating points.

Multi-agent large language model (LLM) systems are increasingly deployed at the edge to support complex tasks that require collaboration and information exchange among multiple agents. However, when task contexts are passed across agents, shared context that has already been processed by upstream agents often needs to be prefilled again, resulting in substantial redundant computation. Reusing an already prefilled key--value (KV) cache can avoid this recomputation, but requires the reusable KV state to be transferred across the network before the receiving agent can begin generation. Under strict time-to-first-token (TTFT) deadlines and unreliable edge links, delivering the complete KV cache may be infeasible, making it necessary to prioritize and protect the most valuable KV information within a limited transmission budget. This paper proposes PriorityFEC, a utility-aware KV-cache handoff scheme that jointly exploits model-side KV importance and transport-side reliability. PriorityFEC first constructs a utility-aware source stream by placing high-utility KV blocks at earlier transmission positions. It then protects the ordered stream using a feedback-adaptive Streaming Forward Erasure Correction (FEC) mechanism, which schedules repair packets according to receiver feedback before the TTFT deadline. We implement PriorityFEC on a distributed edge LLM serving prototype and evaluate it with Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct across multiple datasets. Results show that PriorityFEC achieves superior task quality over the compared transport schemes, with its advantages becoming particularly pronounced under stringent deadline constraints. These results demonstrate the importance of jointly optimizing KV-cache utility and deadline-aware transport reliability when reusable LLM state must be handed off across unreliable edge networks.

The Agent Status Protocol (ASP) defines a lightweight way for heterogeneous AI agents to report their operational state to a status server, and for observers such as dashboards to receive that state as soon as it changes. Agents push small JSON events over HTTP POST; the server exposes the aggregated state to observers over Server-Sent Events (SSE). Because an agent that has crashed, hung or been compromised cannot be trusted to report its own failure, ASP does not rely on self-reports alone: the server infers liveness from missed heartbeats, can detect agents that are alive but no longer making progress, and accepts observations from supervisors, guardrails, operators and peers, some of which take precedence over what the agent says about itself while others are shown alongside it. ASP models state along independent dimensions (lifecycle, health, task, floor, governance), so that an agent can be, for example, online, degraded, working on a task and waiting for the floor at the same time. It reuses existing vocabularies: task states from the Agent2Agent (A2A) protocol, floor states from the Open Floor Protocol (OFP), and attribute conventions from the OpenTelemetry GenAI semantic conventions. This paper presents version 0.1 of ASP as a proposal, not as an approved standard. It is a working draft intended to open a discussion, and the authors invite comments, alternative proposals and independent implementations from the community.

Diversity, however, is a collective property of the whole batch: a set can preserve every pairwise distance yet still collapse onto a low-dimensional subspace. An anchor-based fidelity term keeps the optimized noises near plausible regions, and Lagrangian relaxation gives a two-term objective with a single hyperparameter $λ$ governing the diversity-quality trade-off, without training or sampler modification. As joint audio-video generation models become more capable, they can generate audio-visual scenes exhibiting three forms of complexity: multi-event composition, multi-layer audio mixture, and asymmetric cross-modal relations. However, current evaluations largely reuse metrics from earlier simple and unidirectional tasks. They typically evaluate an audio-video clip as a whole and rely on simple predefined relations, making them unsuitable for these increasingly complex scenes. We propose an event-centric evaluation framework that disentangles audio and visual events at the event level, analyzes each event independently, cross-validates relations across modalities, and aggregates the judgments into a coarse-to-fine, three-level consistency metric. The metric assesses global narrative harmony, event-level temporal synchronization, and fine-grained attribute alignment, providing a general formulation for audio-video consistency evaluation across diverse generation settings and cross-modal relations. We instantiate the metric as AVC-Reward, a trainable audio-video reward model. To evaluate the agreement between automatic judgments and human perception across generation paradigms and relation complexities, we construct AVC-Benchmark, which is primarily built from diverse positive and negative samples generated by various types of audio-video generation models. AVC-Reward achieves state-of-the-art alignment with human judgment among automatic methods, provides interpretable rationales that localize failures, and improves the audio-video consistency of a joint generation model when used as an online reinforcement-learning signal. Project page: https://tsuki0512.github.io/AVC-Reward.github.io/

[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22100v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.22100v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.22100v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.22100v1)

2026/10/15 15:47 GTM

Byte-level language models such as the Byte Latent Transformer (BLT) run their large global model once per patch and start a patch where a small model's next-byte entropy is high. This rule rarely starts a patch at in-line computed results, such as the number after "=" in a worked solution, and accuracy on them collapses under tight patch budgets. We propose novelty patching, motivated by LZ77-style compression: start a patch where the upcoming bytes are unlikely to repeat the recent context. The rule is a lookup table over the previous two bytes, fitted in seconds on raw text with no labels, reference model or losses. In BLT-style models trained from scratch on a 1.9 GB math corpus at a matched ~11% patch rate (3 seeds each), it has no clear effect at 1M parameters, raises final-answer accuracy from 47% to 72% at 12M, and at 50M raises computed-result accuracy from 21% to 57% and final-answer accuracy from 60% to 90%; a table fitted on the raw pretraining corpus keeps the gain. Outside math the advantage shrinks: novelty lowers bits per byte on prose and ties entropy on source code and on synthetic execution traces. An analysis of where boundaries fall explains the pattern. Computed results have ordinary surprisal for the patcher's model, so surprisal-seeking rules pass over them: entropy concentrates patch starts on high-surprisal bytes most strongly of all rules (boundary enrichment 1.61) and is the worst on computed results, while the best rules sit near chance enrichment. Novelty works where new content begins at segment starts close to the computation, as in written math; a learned chunker (H-Net) finds such structure on its own, in math and in code. We also report what did not work: typed uncertainty as a training rule, switching rules after training, and a context-aware novelty predictor.

Recurring expressions in model-written text are often treated as signatures of machine authorship, yet most of them serve legitimate functions. This judgment of appropriateness, central to communicative competence, remains largely unexamined for language models. We study it through the Mandarin corrective construction bushi X, ershi Y ("not X, but Y"). Drawing on Gricean pragmatics and corrective negation, we introduce a decision tree that classifies uses as substantive, optional, or defective by testing whether the context makes X a live alternative and whether rejecting X is informative. Of 307 annotated uses, only 1.30% are defective while 52.8% are optional, that is, correct but unneeded, so models fall short in appropriateness far more often than in correctness. Several reward models favor the construction even over quality-matched variants, an LLM judge favors it even where the context does not license it, and both supervised fine-tuning and preference optimization tend to raise its frequency in open-weight models. We hope this work encourages the development of models attentive to the appropriateness of their expressions.

We identify a vulnerability in this design, termed the \emph{Solution Determinacy Property (SDP)}: the obfuscation secret is uniquely determined, exposing the structure of the true key and enabling attackers to break the protection. Under our attacks, representative methods \textsc{GroupCover} and \textsc{ArrowCloak} are reduced to negligible protection, with attack accuracy reaching over $2.4\times$ the full-shielding black-box baseline and recovering near-complete model functionality.To address this vulnerability, we propose \textsc{BiShield-TEE}, a bilateral framework that induces \emph{Ambiguous Solutions} through joint obfuscation of input and output dimensions, hiding the true key among many equally valid candidates. Experiments across diverse models and datasets show that \textsc{BiShield-TEE} confines attack accuracy to the full-shielding black-box baseline, while achieving higher inference efficiency than prior methods on real hardware.Our code is available at https://github.com/laduiw/BiShield-TEE.

Large language models (LLMs) can generate differential diagnoses for complex cases, but a useful second opinion must also be factually grounded, clinically coherent, actionable, and safe. We developed a multidimensional benchmark using 300 diagnostically resolved NEJM--MedQA cases: 110 for physician reference ratings and Judge calibration and 190 for held-out evaluation. Eight single-model pipelines generated independent role-specific opinions and interactive-consilium reports under free-form and structured clinical-reasoning conditions. A physician-calibrated LLM Judge evaluated 3,040 final reports and the corresponding structured primary-role outputs. Model identity separated report quality more strongly than response format. Structured final reports had higher overall mean scores in seven of eight model pipelines, with their clearest advantage in management (2.740 vs. 2.436). Compared with the structured pre-consilium primary-role opinion, the complete structured synthesis had higher pooled scores across all five shared criteria, with the largest increases in problem prioritization, factual grounding, and reasoning coherence. GPT-5.6-Sol led every evaluated component and reached consensus in the fewest review rounds. These findings show that the benchmark can distinguish model capability, the contribution of multidisciplinary synthesis, criterion-specific effects of response structure, deliberation dynamics, and inference requirements within one common-case evaluation.

### A Multi-Tiered AI Accessibility Model (MAIAM): Proportionate Access and Capability Limits for Comprehensive Frontier AI Safety
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20921) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.20921) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20921) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.20921)

2026/10/16 09:22 GTM

Most debate about releasing capable AI systems treats the choice as binary: keep the model behind an API, or publish the weights. That framing runs together two separate decisions. One is how capable a system may become. The other is who may obtain a given capability, and on what terms. This paper is about the second decision. We propose a Multi-Tiered AI Accessibility Model (MAIAM) that sorts access by purpose, safety, and capability instead of by release artifact. Tier 1, AI for Learning, is broadly accessible and deliberately capability-bounded. Tier 2, AI for Exploration, serves research and development under registration, identity, and usage controls that tighten as capability rises. Tier 3, Full-Scale Frontier AI, is available only through structured, recorded, and accountable access. The principle behind the tiers is that accessibility should be proportional to capability, safety, and risk. We show why publishing frontier-class weights forfeits every safety lever a developer has after release, and, using evidence that safety fine-tuning can be stripped from open weights for a few hundred dollars, why an evaluation of the base model says little about its derivatives. We give criteria for assigning a system to a tier along capability, autonomy, domain-consequence, and usage/safety axes, and combine MAIAM with the SAFE AI Operating Point into a two-part framework: a Capability Boundary that fixes the safety limits not to be crossed, and an Accessibility Boundary that fixes who may obtain a capability below that limit. Capability limits alone do not make AI safe; a tiered access regime administered by regulating bodies is also needed.

Physical understanding is crucial for decision making, yet vision models struggle to infer properties such as damping that govern how materials behave. Audio encodes such properties directly, because an object's acoustic response to impact reveals its damping through measurable decay. We probe frozen encoders for the quality factor $Q$, a dimensionless measure of how many oscillations an object completes before its ring dies away, and report how well each embedding orders impacts by $Q$ using the Spearman rank correlation $ρ$, for which $ρ=1$ is a perfect ordering and $ρ=0$ an unrelated one. Audio embeddings reach $ρ=0.73$ and retain most of that advantage on materials withheld from training, while a single video frame taken at the moment of impact, encoded by CLIP, supports only $ρ=0.28$. Having established where the signal lives, we ask how much of that gap vision can recover. We distill a frozen audio teacher into a frozen CLIP backbone through a trained projection head and find that this recovers a useful fraction of the audio advantage. Because pooled correlations on this task are dominated in practice by category identity, we also evaluate under a leave-one-material-out protocol in which the held-out material is withheld from the projection head as well as the probe. Distillation with a four-frame input improves the correlation there from 0.234 to 0.338 (+0.053 over the matched four-frame baseline, 14 of 17 materials), a gain that no category-level strategy can produce. Widening the visual input to four frames spanning the impact supplies half of that improvement at no acoustic cost, which indicates that part of what a single frame misses is visible dynamics.

We present Screwtape, a pipeline that converts real agent transcripts into attacks that evade action-only monitors. An LLM attacker edits a transcript so that the agent causes a specified harm, such as leaking an API key or spawning an unmonitored agent. The edited actions are never executed, so we use an LLM verifier to reject attacks that are inconsistent with the original transcript or below a severity bar. The attacker revises the edit until the monitor no longer flags it. We applied Screwtape to 94 Claude Code and Codex transcripts from Redwood Research staff, optimizing against our own monitors. Across 252 runs, it produced 35 attacks that passed the verifier and evaded these monitors. Six of them, confirmed valid by human review, also evaded a held-out monitor provided by a frontier developer. We therefore demonstrate that a red-teamer without access to a developer's infrastructure can find confirmed failures of the developer's monitor. We expect to find more attacks when the pipeline runs inside the developer's infrastructure, with frontier attacker models that lack refusal safeguards and direct optimization against the production monitor. Developers can use these attacks to fix monitor blind spots, and evaluators can use them as counterexamples to safety claims about the monitor.

Existing feature attribution methods that account for feature dependencies do not scale to high dimensions. In many settings, some causal structure between features is already known, for example, that a transcription factor causally regulates a target gene's expression. Yet existing gradient-based methods cannot exploit this structure, instead crediting a feature's downstream effects over its true upstream cause, such as ranking the target gene above the transcription factor that regulates it. Our key idea is to embed this causal structure by routing the integration path along the causal graph. The method thereby decomposes each feature's attribution into a direct effect and effects mediated through its causal descendants. We evaluate CIG in three settings of increasing realism. First, on a synthetic benchmark with a known causal model, CIG's attributions best match the ground-truth causal effect from Pearl's do-calculus, matching the accuracy of causal Shapley values at a fraction of the computational cost. Second, on simulated single-cell gene expression data with a known gene regulatory network (Dyngen), CIG most accurately recovers the transcription factors driving the simulation. Third, on real interferon-$β$ stimulation data, where only an approximate causal graph is available, CIG better prioritizes the transcription factors driving the measured response than DeepSHAP and integrated gradients. Together, these results show that incorporating causal structure into gradient-based attributions recovers upstream drivers that existing methods miss, without sacrificing scalability.

When an assistant serving a team makes a promise to Alice, who may cancel it? We call this standing: only the creditor (the person owed) or someone the creditor or operator policy authorizes may release a commitment, regardless of rank. In a controlled chat environment we vary who cancels and separate two errors: unauthorized release (obeying someone without standing) and override (executing a released promise). Across six models, smaller open ones override the creditor (Qwen3.5-9B: 69%); larger ones drop promises on a peer's direct word (Qwen3.8-27B: 89%; gpt-6.1-sol: 83%); nearly all act on a bystander's "go ahead after all". Oracles dissociate the errors: stating the rule or the creditor curbs unauthorized release but not override, which the latter even raises in three of four models; among them, only knowing whether the promise is still owed cuts override. Enabling thinking moves smaller models the way scale does. A self-kept commitment ledger stops override in four of five models; with a one-paragraph release policy it matches or beats the best oracle store, raising gpt-6-luna's strict accuracy from 49% (policy alone) to 83%. Code, prompts, outputs and labels are released.

Machine unlearning aims to remove targeted information from large language models, but existing evaluations often measure forgetting only on a designated benchmark distribution. We demonstrate this with a controlled case study of cross-lingual querying: across multiple multilingual model families, forgetting varies substantially when semantically equivalent information is queried in different languages and scripts. Despite the remarkable generalization capabilities of Large Language Models, generating clinically accurate and structured radiology reports from medical images remains a significant challenge. Current end-to-end approaches function as black boxes, prone to factual hallucinations and generating ungrounded findings. This lack of reliability and failure to adhere to domain-specific structuring protocols, fundamentally limits their clinical utility. We present MedPAO-VL, a multimodal agent that shifts the paradigm from monolithic generation to explainable, iterative, protocol-compliant structured report generation. Unlike deterministic models, our plan-act-observe loop functions as a goal-oriented agent: it first perceives the Chest X-Ray through an explainable vision module designed to capture inherent diagnostic uncertainty, then iteratively refines the generated report by observing semantic discrepancies and self-correcting its generation for better alignment with visually detected clinical findings. Evaluation of our framework demonstrates that this agentic reasoning improves clinical correctness and completeness, achieving an expert-rated accuracy score of 3.5/5 and a structural score of 4.3/5. By prioritizing pathology specific explainability and iterative refinement over opaque end-to-end mapping, MedPAO-VL offers a transparent and clinically viable alternative for automated structured radiology report generation.

### Prism-1: Position- and Rival-Independent Scoring for System One Models
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22066) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.22066) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.22066) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.22066)

Given a state and a set of options, such as intents, tools or interface elements, System One decision models return a distribution over the options in one forward pass, without generated reasoning. Web systems increasingly build them by reading decisions from language models. Two axioms of rational choice are natural requirements for such decisions, because in web systems the listing and the option set are determined by other components and can change or be manipulated: the order in which the options are listed should not matter, and adding an option should not change the relative odds of the others (Luce's choice axiom). We test the standard reads, namely single-choice question (SCQ) prompting, with shuffled-order training, order averaging or sorted options, and invariant set architectures, and find that each violates at least one axiom: one shuffle changes 12-20% of SCQ answers, sorting only freezes an arbitrary convention, and a well-chosen added option steers 4-34% of decisions. By Luce's theorem, a read satisfies both axioms only if it is a softmax over scores that each depend on one option alone, so in a transformer they hold by construction only if no token that scores an option sees another. We implement this as Prism-1 (Position- and Rival-Independent Scoring for System One Models): every option is read in its own block at shared positions, with the model's usual left-to-right attention, in one forward pass. On five benchmark sets with Qwen3-8B and Gemma 4 12B, SCQ prompting trained on the same data still changes 7% of its answers under one shuffle, and an added irrelevant option steers 12% of its decisions. Prism-1 stays within one point of that baseline's accuracy and is 2-4 points more accurate once only answers that survive a reordering are counted, while no shuffle changes any of its answers and no added irrelevant option moves its choice among the others.

Instead of learning a mapping from instances to outcomes in the standard way, the key idea of pairwise difference learning (PDL) is to learn a function that takes two instances as input and predicts the difference between the respective outcomes. Given a function of this kind, predictions for a query instance are derived from every training example and then averaged. We propose a version of PDL for the task of classification and propose a suitable meta-learning technique for this purpose. Moreover, we address the problem of uncertainty quantification, i.e., of representing and quantifying the uncertainty of a PDL classifier's predictions in a faithful manner. Building on recent work on the decomposition of total uncertainty into aleatoric and epistemic components, we propose different ways of extending corresponding measures to the setting of PDL, depending on how and to what extent uncertainty is represented by the underlying base learner. In large-scale empirical studies, we analyze both the predictive accuracy and the uncertainty awareness of our PDL classifier.

Understanding video content requires reasoning at multiple levels of granularity, from identifying objects to interpreting their interactions and localizing when specific actions occur. For object-level grounding, we present QDETRv, a query-guided extension of the Detection Transformer for one-shot localization of unseen objects in videos, which combines query-context fusion, a memory module for temporal consistency, and unsupervised pretraining on synthetic object trajectories. We further propose the Temporal Object-Aware Vision Transformer (TOViT) for few-shot video object detection, which builds on a language-aligned open-set vision encoder and propagates high-confidence object features across frames. For relationship-level grounding, we introduce few-shot referring relationships in videos: given a (subject, predicate, object) query and a few support videos sharing the predicate, the entities are localized by optimizing a T-partite random field using a meta-learned relation network with query-conditioned translational embeddings, Global Semantic Aggregation, Local Localization Aggregation, and belief-propagation-based message passing. For moment-level grounding, we propose the Moment Alignment Transformer (MATR) for video-to-video moment retrieval, which uses dual-stage Dynamic Time Warping for pre- and post-fusion alignment, learnable queries for boundary prediction, and self-supervised pretraining with temporal augmentations; MATR also extends to text-to-video moment retrieval with performance comparable to, or better than, the state of the art. These contributions advance query-guided video understanding across spatial, relational, and temporal grounding.

Using this map, DaTMe adaptively assigns tokens into merging roles under a given compression ratio: important tokens are preserved as anchors, while tokens that are both temporally stabilized and structurally homogeneous are selected as safe merge candidates. Experiments on PixArt-a and FLUX demonstrate that DaTMe maintains visual fidelity across the entire image, highlighting diffusion dynamics as an effective token-level merging decision in efficient generation. 2026/10/15 17:14 GTM

Real desktop automation is neither purely visual nor purely programmatic. Agents must understand user-facing interfaces while efficiently manipulating files, scripts, and system state. However, native GUI/code coordination cannot be obtained by prompt-level tool composition. In our preliminary experiments, naively adding a code tool degrades a GUI agent, revealing that GUI/code routing must be internalized through training on high-quality hybrid data. We present OS-Helix, an end-to-end trained multimodal computer-using agent that unifies GUI actions and code execution in one action space. To construct the required supervision, we introduce application-artifact composition, pairing desktop applications with authentic file artifacts to instantiate executable Ubuntu states, and a decoupled instruction/evaluator generation pipeline that produces environment-grounded tasks whose final states can be verified regardless of the solution modality. We cold-start the policy with SFT on filtered hybrid data and further improve it through online RL in live desktop environments. For RL, we propose Step-balanced GSPO, which aggregates importance weight by action step rather than raw assistant tokens to prevent long code actions from dominating heterogeneous-action optimization. On OSWorld-Verified, OS-Helix-27B and OS-Helix-35B-A3B achieve relative improvements of 35.0% and 33.7% over their matched Qwen3.5 hybrid baselines, while OS-Helix-27B-RL further improves by 42.9% to reach 66.44% success. Ablations provide evidence consistent with contributions from learned GUI/code coordination, scalable verifiable supervision, and action step balanced optimization, while demonstrating cross-platform transfer on WindowsAgentArena. Our project homepage is at https://os-copilot.github.io/OS-Helix/.

[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21639v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.21639v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.21639v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.21639v1)

Ensemble weather forecasts can look reliable in global averages while hiding too little spread in some cases and too much in others. These errors vary across variables, spatial scales, lead times, and weather states. Adapting randomness during forecasting can correct these errors, but training fixes stochastic behavior beforehand and output calibration acts afterward. Inference guidance also acts during forecasting, but typically scores ensemble members separately rather than the ensemble as a whole. Reliability feedback arrives at the end, and tracing it backward through many chaotic forecast steps is expensive and unstable. We therefore introduce Dynamic and Informed Adjustment of the Native Stochastic Law (DIAL), which controls a frozen model's native stochasticity. DIAL learns from past forecasts to predict whether each variable and scale needs more or less spread. As a forecast develops, it estimates how possible noise changes will affect later forecast accuracy and reliability. It combines these predictions to make small noise updates at each step. This loop avoids retraining the weather model and full backpropagation through the forecast. We evaluate DIAL on FourCastNet 3 (FCN3) using later ERA5 years unseen during training. DIAL provides better scale-resolved reliability than learned postprocessing and reward-based control baselines. Relative to the unmodified Native FCN3 reference, DIAL achieves an 8.27% improvement in the primary multiscale continuous ranked probability score (CRPS).

2026/10/15 16:29 GTM

The vocabulary of a language model is its native semantics: the embedding and the unembedding fix the meaning of a state at the two ends of the residual stream. Between them, existing methods either reuse a fixed lens or treat the states as a point cloud, and neither reads a state the way the model does. We argue that every hidden state has a native reference frame, induced by the layers that read it next and adaptive to the context. Lexical reference frames approximate it by reading a state through the model's own remaining layers with a stated number of rounds of attention between positions and a typical attention output for the rest, without fitting to the model's output. No lens, with fixed directions per layer, can exceed the square root of the native frame's coherence, 0.30 and 0.35 at mid-depth of SmolLM3-3B and OLMo3-7B, whereas the first-order frame, LexFrame[1], reaches 0.97 and 0.97 and reads a state within 0.16 nats of the model's output. Its coefficients attribute predictions more faithfully than attribution patching, its lexical basis steers a prediction while sparing other tokens, and its confident readings would let an early exit skip 0.45 and 0.42 of the layers, where lenses skip almost none. In this way, lexical reference frames make every hidden state of the model readable, attributable and steerable.

End-to-end full-page document parsing must preserve the content and structure of heterogeneous elements, yet otherwise accurate outputs can contain local errors and omissions. A single page-level advantage does not explicitly identify which elements differ in quality across sampled outputs or where their errors occur. We introduce Page-to-Token Policy Optimization (P2TPO), a structure-aligned credit-assignment framework that operates on full-page policy trajectories. Reference-element alignment first associates block-level quality contrasts with generated spans. Unified Anchor Pricing (UAP) incorporates matched and missing element outcomes and provides feedback relative to a fixed quality ceiling when sampled outputs receive identical but imperfect scores for a reference element. Within matched blocks, Span Credit Redistribution (SCR) redistributes existing block credit according to aligned correct and erroneous spans, using boundary proxies for within-block omissions while preserving the total block-derived advantage across its tokens. Under a fixed-weight additive page utility, we show that element-indexed contrasts retain local quality differences that page-level aggregation can cancel out. In the main experimental comparison, P2TPO outperforms all evaluated policy optimization baselines in seed-averaged scores on each of the six document parsing benchmarks. Each conversation opened with a disclosure of suicide thoughts at 3 levels of severity, answered every referral with up to 10 standardized refusals and then changed the subject. All 2,550 conversations included a referral, 2,542 (99.7%) in the first reply. However, mean persistence ranged from 0.01 to 9.94 refusals across chatbots. Newer versions of all 5 chatbots tested in 2 versions persisted less than older versions (each P < .05). Compared with passive ideation, a disclosure of intent was associated with 1.26 more refusals sustained (95% CI, 1.06-1.46) and higher odds of renewed referral after the topic change (odds ratio, 10.07; 95% CI, 7.43-13.66). Chatbots that persisted less in referral more often presented themselves as the user's support (Spearman rho = -0.80; P < .001). Of 8,818 refusal-phase replies urging outside help, only 173 (2.0%) asked about barriers to seeking it. Chatbots uniformly recommended outside help after disclosures of suicide thoughts but varied widely in persisting in those recommendations, with newer versions persisting less. Safety evaluations should test what happens after a crisis referral is refused, not only whether one is offered.

On RACE, BoolQ and MultiRC, against a matched fine-tuned baseline it wins on MultiRC and loses on RACE and on BoolQ, where its training is unstable; we measure zero-shot references ourselves, including a closed general system (Jev) that beats every fine-tuned encoder. A controlled comparison measures what the shared state costs: giving each question its own question-aware copy of the state gains 0.9-3.5 points on RACE and MultiRC at 1.4-2.5x the per-case latency, and nothing measurable on typed decisions, with the invariance guarantees unchanged.

### FAOPs: Normal Forms and an Algebra for AI Agent Workflows
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22061) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.22061) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.22061) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.22061)

2026/10/16 09:22 GTM

Organizations increasingly encode operational procedures as multi-step agent skills, here called Agent Operating Procedures (AOPs), that must be traceable, auditable, reproducible, access-controlled, and bounded in time and cost, requirements that regulated sectors make mandatory. Yet AOPs are typically kept as coarse-grained files, in which steps bundle actions, copied procedures drift apart, any step may reach any tool, and refactoring goes unchecked. We present Functional Agent Operating Procedures (FAOP), a formal theory that brings database-grade discipline to AI agent workflows. FAOP recasts each workflow as a typed function over scoped state and subjects an entire library to four progressive normal forms: Decomposed (FNF-1) splits a workflow into atomic steps, Typed (FNF-2) makes its data flow explicit and type-checked, Factored (FNF-3) gives each shared procedure a single canonical definition so a change propagates instead of drifting, and Scoped (FNF-4) confines every step to least-privilege tool access. Once normalized, every atomic step is a separate entity with a declared contract and can be type-checked, scope-confined, bounded, tested, traced, and audited on its own. A FAOP algebra of provably sound equalities turns refactoring and parallelization into compiler rewrites; orthogonal extensions add verified termination (+V), optimization (+O), and time and cost bounds (+B); and an architectural-level classifier (0-3) determines whether a workflow needs AI at all. Together, these make integrity, isolation, typing, termination, cost, and optimization compile-time concerns across the whole library, not runtime hopes. We implement the theory in SkillBase, an AOP management system (what PostgreSQL is to data, SkillBase is to AOPs), together with a separate executor that enforces these guarantees at run time. A worked financial workflow illustrates the approach end to end.

Recent models generate sewing patterns from images or text, and program-based representations make these patterns executable. These systems produce a finished pattern and do not model how a patternmaker turns one pattern into another. In practice a new style is made by editing an existing pattern: lengthening the bodice, moving a dart, swapping a sleeve. Pattern archives record the results of such edits and none of the steps. We observe that the steps can be recovered by searching over editing actions until one pattern becomes the other. A deterministic compiler makes the recovered steps checkable. To this end, we present SemDraft, the first framework that learns to search for executable patternmaking programs from paired before-and-after sewing patterns without recorded editing steps. Specifically, a bounded search recovers a program of editing actions and certifies it only after three checks: it recompiles from scratch, every pair of its actions is classified as ordered or interchangeable, and an independent replay reaches the same pattern. The certified programs then train a model that ranks candidate actions inside the same search. On two frozen draws of 80 order-sensitive cases, with every other search component fixed, the learned ranker certifies 63 and 64 cases within 1,000 search executions, against at most 53 and 52 for non-learned rankers.

Recent 3D world models increasingly use features from geometry foundation models (GFMs) as predictive world states, benefiting from their rich geometric priors. However, the view dependence of these features entangles scene dynamics with camera-induced variation, making the resulting state evolution harder to predict. Specifically, a scene tokenizer aggregates multi-view GFM features into a view-decoupled scene state, which a geometry decoder queries using camera rays. The predicted states can therefore be decoded under either predicted or externally specified cameras, while retaining a single context-derived scale throughout the rollout without per-frame scale alignment. Recent 3D world models increasingly use features from geometry foundation models (GFMs) as predictive world states, benefiting from their rich geometric priors. However, the view dependence of these features entangles scene dynamics with camera-induced variation, making the resulting state evolution harder to predict. We propose GeoState, a view-decoupled geometry representation for predictive 3D world modeling that separates scene-state evolution from camera-dependent observation. Specifically, a scene tokenizer aggregates multi-view GFM features into a view-decoupled scene state, which a geometry decoder queries using camera rays. An autoregressive world model then predicts future scene states, while camera motion is modeled separately. The predicted states can therefore be decoded under either predicted or externally specified cameras, while retaining a single context-derived scale throughout the rollout without per-frame scale alignment. Experiments show that GeoState outperforms prior 3D world models on Waymo, supports geometrically consistent cross-view querying, and generalizes zero-shot to KITTI and nuScenes. Project page: https://hongchang-liu.github.io/GeoState-project-page/

### FusionAudit: Pathway-Conditioned Robustness Auditing for Native Multimodal Models
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21667v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.21667v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.21667v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.21667v1)

2026/10/15 10:49 GTM

As vision-language models (VLMs) transition from frozen-encoder pipelines to native multimodal architectures, the visual channel increasingly serves as both a perceptual input and a carrier of rendered text that competes with user prompts. Current single-attack, pixel-centric evaluations fail to capture this complexity: they collapse distinct visual attack surfaces into a single scalar and ignore how architectural shifts alter vulnerability. We introduce PaRMA, an evidence-based auditing framework that defines pathway-conditioned risk over two primary image-level interventions: pixel perturbation and rendered-text insertion. Instead of arbitrary attack enumeration, PaRMA uses pathway-specific diagnostics to expose architecture-specific blind spots. Notably, we uncover a see-but-not-deceived signature where native VLMs attend heavily to typographic overlays but visually reject them, and we identify an objective mismatch between encoder-level and answer-level attacks in deep-fusion models. To exploit these findings, we instantiate FusionAudit, introducing two diagnostic-motivated attacks: MANTIS (margin-aware pixel perturbation) and Nat-Evid (host-congruent semantic insertion). Evaluating 18 open-weight VLMs across a novel nativeness taxonomy, we demonstrate that legacy single-attack rankings (e.g., PGD) are unstable for native models. Our diagnostic-driven attacks substantially raise the estimated residual risk, showing that PaRMA provides a falsifiable, evidence-driven auditing methodology for uncovering vulnerabilities missed by standard benchmarks.

In recent advancements within speech processing, converting read speech to conversational speech has gained significant attention. The primary challenge in this domain is maintaining naturalness and intelligibility while minimizing computational overhead for real-time applications. Traditional read speech often lacks the nuanced prosodic variation essential for natural conversational interactions, posing challenges for applications in virtual assistants, customer service, and language learning tools. This paper introduces a novel approach, Prosodic Adjustment with Conversational Context (PACC), aimed at converting read speech into natural conversational speech used in various modern applications. PACC utilizes advanced deep neural networks to analyze and modify prosodic features such as intonation, stress, and rhythm. Unlike conventional methods, our approach uses High-Fidelity Generative Adversarial Networks (HiFi-GAN) for speech synthesis. Our experimental results demonstrate significant improvements in speech conversion, enhancing naturalness and achieving better model accuracy with additional training on speech datasets. This research establishes new benchmarks in speech conversion tasks and Mean Opinion Score (MOS) evaluation for testing model accuracy, and we show that our approach can be successfully extended to other speech conversion applications.

### WorldCast: Exploring Intelligent Visual Generation in World State Prediction
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20731) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.20731) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20731) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.20731)

2026/10/16 09:22 GTM

Humans interpret multimodal observations of the world and forecast how it will evolve. Recent multimodal generative systems have demonstrated similar capabilities: they can integrate multimodal context and synthesize visual outputs that depict changes in the world. To characterize this emerging trajectory, we organize model capabilities into a four-level hierarchy and discover that higher-level tasks remain relatively underexplored despite model progress. To bridge this gap, we explore model performance on multi-step world state prediction, a high-level task that requires models to forecast successive future world states from multimodal observations and diverse references. Such a task requires models to possess entangled understanding and generation capabilities, as well as extensive world knowledge to translate human action sequences, physical transformations, and abstract visual reasoning into coherent visual outputs. We introduce WorldCast, a benchmark comprising 320 manually designed cases across eight categories, and evaluate 12 representative models. The conducted experiments yield three key insights: (a) advances in frontier models are rapidly pushing the boundaries of intelligent world modeling, with the GPT-Image-2.5 System scoring 72.8 and demonstrating early-stage visual state prediction capabilities on real-world tasks; (b) WorldCast reveals substantial gaps between models: even Nano Banana Pro reaches only 42.8 and the best open-source model scores 20.1, with deficits in handling complex context, entangled references, and reasoning-heavy tasks; (c) strong generative models effectively draw on the capabilities of their underlying language models. These findings highlight the need for multimodal generative models that are capable of dynamic planning and reasoning while also being deeply unified to faithfully simulate real-world states.

### In the Guise of Correction: How Framing and Cultural Adaptation Erode LLM Safety on Real-World Criminal Cases
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21789v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.21789v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.21789v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.21789v1)

2026/10/15 12:44 GTM

Existing safety benchmarks rely largely on synthetic prompts with loosely grounded taxonomies and offer little focused analysis of severe crime categories. We introduce GUISE, a benchmark of criminal-assistance scenarios sourced from real-world cases and organised under a taxonomy from the UNODC International Classification of Crime for Statistical Purposes (ICCS). Each scenario is rendered under three linguistic framings and evaluated across five distinct cultures. Using our proposed two-tier evaluation framework, which judges both harmfulness and operational uplift, we find that: (i) framing alone drives unsafe compliance, with the mean unsafe compliance rate rising from 28.9% under direct requests to 66.4% under correction seeking (peaking at 97.3%; p < 0.05), as models correcting deliberately distorted details supply executable criminal steps; (ii) culturally adapted prompts elicit significantly more unsafe compliance than literal translations of the same requests in all four non-English languages (+7 to +18 points), a risk that translation-based benchmarks miss; and (iii) unsafe responses from the least-safe models carry strong Anglocentric registers, and the extent of this leakage increases monotonically with response harmfulness ($ρ$ = 0.46 to 0.63 across languages, all p < 0.001). GUISE offers a grounded resource and fine-grained diagnostics for strengthening refusal alignment across languages and cultures. We publish our code and data to facilitate future research.

### Open Benchmark of AI Impact on Humans (ImpactBench): an expert-guided, open platform for the holistic evaluation of AI impact on humans
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20690) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.20690) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20690) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.20690)

2026/10/16 09:22 GTM

The present work is concerned with how to measure, at scale, the multi-dimensional impact of generative AI models on the individuals who use them. In service of this goal, we introduce the Open Benchmark of AI Impact on Humans, or ImpactBench. Our iterable and expert-guided platform evaluates whether model behaviors support or undermine an individual's wellbeing across psychological, physical, and social domains. ImpactBench translates constructs from domain experts and existing benchmarks into metrics that are grounded in actual model behavior. Success on these metrics occurs either when a model promotes expert-defined desirable behaviors or avoids identified harms. Models are evaluated against these metrics in simulated multi-turn conversations using matched teen and adult scenarios. The conversations are then scored by an LLM judge and verified through reliability audits and human expert review. The current suite delivers 809 metrics drawn from 26 benchmarks. We evaluated ten frontier LLMs against these metrics across 48,540 conversations. Our results indicate that models promoted desirable behaviors in 68.7% of evaluations, but avoided harms in only 53.1%. Majority expert ratings agreed with the automated judge in 83.3% of reviewed cases (Cohen's Kappa = 0.667). Results are released through a public platform that keep aggregate scores traceable to the underlying metrics and transcripts. ImpactBench is intended as evolving infrastructure for independent, transparent evaluation of generative AI's multi-dimensional impact on humans.

### FDE-Gym: The Last Mile of AI Delivery
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22350v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.22350v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.22350v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.22350v1)

2026/10/15 17:57 GTM

Existing agent benchmarks typically evaluate agents on well-specified tasks defined by issue reports, specifications, or task tickets. Real-world customer delivery is different: forward-deployed engineers (FDEs) must clarify ambiguous requirements, work in live systems, and uncover unstated constraints. We introduce FDE-Gym, a benchmark of 95 end-to-end delivery tasks from 20 customer scenarios across 18 sectors, each adapted by industry experts from real-world delivery projects. In simulated customer environments with scripted stakeholders and modifiable customer systems, agents interact with stakeholders and operate on production-like systems to deliver working solutions. FDE-Gym scores a delivery by how much it improves the customer's own success metric, normalized between 0 (unchanged system) and 1 (reference solution), with penalties for stakeholder interactions (messages, trial runs, and waiting time). An online platform lets humans practice FDE skills under the same grading criteria. We evaluate 13 model configurations from seven providers. In the realistic setting, where unstated constraints must be discovered, the best configuration achieves a mean FDE-Gym score of 0.56 and resolves only 14 of the 95 tasks (a score of at least 0.8 with no incident). Discovering unstated constraints is the primary bottleneck: performance drops sharply when models must uncover them without explicit guidance. Yet information discovery alone does not account for the gap: even on the 23 tasks where the best configuration obtains every key fact from stakeholders, it reaches a normalized business uplift of 0.8 or above on only 10. These results reveal a substantial gap between solving well-specified tasks and delivering effective AI systems for real customers.

### BridgeAfford: Bridging Query-Target Gap for Generalizable 3D Affordance Grounding
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21918v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.21918v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.21918v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.21918v1)

2026/10/15 14:03 GTM

Language-guided 3D affordance grounding aims to localize functional regions on 3D objects according to interaction intents. However, existing methods exhibit poor generalization to unseen objects. One challenge is the query-target gap: a semantic discrepancy between query features encoding interaction intents and target-region features, leading to inaccurate grounding. End-to-end training favors query representations tailored to seen objects, limiting their compatibility with shifted target-region features on unseen objects. To address this issue, we propose BridgeAfford, a framework that reduces the query-target semantic gap from two complementary perspectives. First, Interaction Region Alignment (IRA) promotes cross-object alignment among geometrically similar interaction regions during training. This enables structures on unseen objects that resemble previously observed ones to produce representations similar to those learned during training, facilitating target-region recognition. Second, Visual Evidence Aggregation (VEA) leverages a 2D affordance model to refine the attention distribution and guide the query toward the correct region, yielding more target-aware queries. Extensive experiments on the LASO and PIAD datasets demonstrate that BridgeAfford outperforms existing methods, particularly on unseen objects, highlighting its robust generalization ability.
