### PremiseCheck: Efficient Fact-Checking of Human and AI Peer Reviews
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21046) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.21046) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.21046) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.21046)

2026/10/16 09:22 GTM

Peer review is under strain from the growth of submissions, and review comments written by humans or AI can misstate what a paper says. We introduce ReviewFact, a benchmark that labels such factual errors in review comments under one definition across NLP, machine learning, and the natural sciences, and that measures cost and latency alongside accuracy. A frontier LLM checks comments well but must read the whole paper for each check, while cheap decision models, which score whether a statement holds in a text, fall far short on whole comments that mix several implicit assertions. PremiseCheck is a framework that combines the two. An LLM rewrites each comment as explicit premises without reading the paper, and a decision model checks each premise against the paper. When each comment is checked on its own, PremiseCheck reaches accuracy comparable to GPT-5.5 across all three fields at about half its cost, and on NLP reviews at a quarter of its cost and less than half its latency.

### WaveLLM: Wavelet-Domain LLM Entropy Modeling for Lossless Image Compression
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22232v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.22232v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.22232v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.22232v1)

2026/10/15 17:12 GTM

Lossless image compression fundamentally relies on accurate entropy modeling, driving a recent shift toward large language model (LLM)-based methods. However, existing approaches mainly emphasize the modeling capacity of LLMs while directly serializing images into pixel or residual sequences, leaving heterogeneous signal patterns entangled within a single prediction stream. In this paper, we argue that effective entropy modeling depends not only on model capacity, but also on the predictability of the input representation. Motivated by this insight, we propose WaveLLM, a wavelet-domain LLM-based entropy model for lossless image compression. By decomposing an image into multi-level wavelet subbands, WaveLLM converts a heterogeneous prediction problem into a set of more structured and statistically homogeneous subproblems that are more amenable to autoregressive probability estimation. To bridge numerical coding symbols with the LLM's native text token space, we introduce a dedicated numerical interface featuring value embeddings and a coefficient prediction head. Furthermore, a task-adaptive prompting strategy is designed to enable a single shared model to adapt to diverse subbands within a unified framework. Built on Llama-3.2-3B with LoRA adaptation, WaveLLM achieves 2.51, 2.24, and 2.27 bpsp on Kodak, CLIC.pro, and DIV2K, respectively, outperforming the PixelCNN-based codec using the same wavelet transform by 0.43, 0.36, and 0.46 bpsp, and surpassing recent LLM-based lossless compressors on all three benchmarks.

### Training-Free Target-Aware Refinement of 3D Gaussian Language Map
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21619v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.21619v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.21619v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.21619v1)

2026/10/15 10:00 GTM

Distilling vision-language features into 3D Gaussian Splatting yields a language map that can be queried with arbitrary text. In deployment, however, a small set of user-specified targets must be reliably located, which current distilled maps often miss, while open-vocabulary queryability for other words must remain intact. We propose a training-free, target-aware refinement of an already-distilled language map. Guided by a text-promptable segmenter, the visual embedding inside each detected target region is blended with the target text embedding, a convex combination whose coefficient sets the amount of injected prior, while non-target regions are left untouched. Injecting too much overwrites appearance cues and general queryability, so we seek, for each target, the smallest coefficient that still lets it win, and we obtain every coefficient in closed form, leaving no hyperparameters to tune. We further identify a failure mode of injection in language-goal localization. Text embeddings share a common component, and injecting them makes the standard cosine read-out query-agnostic, which a centering step in the read-out removes. Across three benchmarks, the refinement consistently improves open-vocabulary segmentation, roughly doubling the base mIoU on Replica, and localizes language goals more reliably than the base map. It also transfers as a plug-in to off-the-shelf field-based and object-level language maps and supports a real-world demonstration in which a legged robot navigates to natural-language goals. Project page and code: https://lab-of-ai-and-robotics.github.io/target-aware-langmap/

### Qwen-Audio-Jev: A Unified Interface for Audio Decisions
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21614) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.21614) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.21614) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.21614)

2026/10/16 09:22 GTM

Large audio language models (LALMs) have made substantial progress in audio understanding. However, in high-throughput applications requiring frequent selection, judgment, and scoring, autoregressive text generation introduces additional computational overhead and response latency. To address this limitation, we introduce Qwen-Audio-Jev by augmenting Qwen-Audio-3.1-ASR-Flash-Next with a dedicated Jev decision head. The model unifies selection, binary judgment, and scoring through three decision primitives: CHOICE, NOUL, and SCORE. Given audio, a natural-language question, and an explicit answer specification, it directly produces a task-conditioned probability distribution or a numerical score through typed decision readouts. Evaluation across 18 labeled configurations covers all three primitives: for CHOICE, Qwen-Audio-Jev achieves accuracies of 78.70% on MMAU-mini and 84.06% on MMSU; for NOUL, it achieves an equal error rate of 6.50% on the VoxCeleb1-O speaker verification benchmark; and for SCORE, the model achieves a normalized mean absolute error of 0.1062 on NISQA overall quality assessment, with Pearson and Spearman correlations of 0.8210 and 0.8074, respectively. Compared with the original Qwen-Audio-3.1-ASR-Flash-Next, Qwen-Audio-Jev achieves an approximately 20x inference speedup. It demonstrates its potential for efficient, high-throughput audio decision-making in applications such as large-scale audio screening, speaker verification, and automated quality assessment.

### Hierarchical Interpretable Multitask Audio Representation Learning: An Analysis of Event-Scene Relationships through the Information Bottleneck
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21520) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.21520) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.21520) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.21520)

2026/10/16 09:22 GTM

Joint Acoustic Scene Classification (ASC) and Audio Tagging (AT) models traditionally rely on unconstrained multihead architectures. However, this design ignores the natural hierarchical dependency between sounds and acoustic scenes. In this work, we investigate a hierarchical (sequential) conditioning paradigm that predicts scene categories directly from intermediate event probabilities. Through a large-scale empirical benchmark with 66 full training runs across three state-of-the-art backbones (PaSST, PANNs, and BEATs) and different loss-weighting settings (beta in [0.0, 1.0]), we show that hierarchical conditioning achieves competitive classification performance while altering multitask optimization dynamics. Unlike multihead baselines, which sequentially solve the easier ASC task before optimizing AT, the hierarchical architecture enables synchronized dual-task learning for specific loss-weighting settings (beta in [0.6, 0.9]). Furthermore, we propose a multi-perspective interpretability framework that combines an analysis of the information bottleneck, manifold geometry, and Formal Concept Analysis (FCA) on a Term-Frequency Inverse-Document-Frequency (TF-IDF) event representation, providing complementary insights into event-scene relationships. Our findings reveal that hierarchical conditioning systematically yields lower conditional entropy, higher mutual information, and superior latent cluster separability (higher Silhouette scores), establishing a more structured and semantically faithful intermediate feature space.

### Name-Bias in Large Language Models
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22053v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.22053v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.22053v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.22053v1)

2026/10/15 15:19 GTM

Names carry demographic indicators such as age, gender, and race, which makes them a subtle but strong target of bias in large language models (LLMs). In this work, we present an evaluation of name-based bias across 6 local large language models spanning multiple providers and parameter scales. Using a fill-in-the-blank task, we prompt these models to choose between pairs of first names drawn from U.S. Social Security Administration datasets and a race classification dataset by Rosenman et al.. Our experimental de- sign isolates age and gender effects through balanced name pairings and sentence templates that share a fixed syntactic structure. Across 300,000 local LLM responses, we find consistent and measurable name bias. Models prefer names that are less stereotypically male, older, popular, and white. Furthermore, we find that name-bias is highly sentence-dependent, but not more than associated stereotypes, and gender is the most significant bias, followed by age, name popularity, and race. Our results demonstrate that name-based bias remains pervasive even in local LLMs, highlighting the necessity for standardized evaluation methods and mitigation strategies.

### Approve What, Exactly? Effect Binding and Trusted Rendering for Agent Step-Up Authorization
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21063) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.21063) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.21063) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.21063)

2026/10/16 09:22 GTM

AI agents now pause for approval before they send money, delete files or email outsiders, and that approval is usually treated as a second factor. We argue it is one only when compromising the agent cannot produce it and when it names exactly what it approves, and we test both conditions. We define twelve attacks on the approval path, including decoy recipients, amount cancellation, card padding, label spoofing, enrollment squatting and threshold splitting, and run them against the Agent Payments Protocol (AP2), the Microsoft action-bound approval design and Model Context Protocol (MCP) elicitation. An audit of ten agent runtimes finds no third-party verifiable approval record, no flood limits where measurable, and post-approval argument changes in four of eight runtimes with measurable binding. As a reference defense we build ASU (Agent Step-Up), a gateway that renders the approval card from a canonical effect descriptor, has a passkey on the phone of the user sign its digest, and executes only the stored call. Tamarin proves its binding, single-use and key-level non-repudiation properties under three stated hypotheses (53 lemmas). Across 27,108 scripted trials and 609 AgentDojo attack pairs, ASU stops substitution, replay and grant stretching, and the trusted card cuts forged-narration attack success from 0.54 to 0.03. With 15 current language models as automated approvers, the trusted card raises the attack catch rate from 0.30 to 0.98, at the cost of refusing 16 percent of harmless near misses, mostly due to a placeholder artifact in our stimuli. A second device adds nothing measurable over a trusted on-host confirmation in these experiments, flooding still succeeds 0.45 to 0.60 of the time against a modeled approver, and approval cannot stop effects that policy already allows. Code, data and proofs: https://github.com/Sports-Vision-Inc/agent-step-up

### OmicsBench: a long-horizon multi-turn benchmark of AI agents on research-study-scale bioinformatics
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21074) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.21074) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.21074) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.21074)

2026/10/16 09:22 GTM

Computational biologists increasingly delegate substantial analyses to AI agents in long-horizon, multi-turn sessions, yet most benchmarks of agents' bioinformatics capabilities pose a single short task with a single-shot prompt. We present OmicsBench, a long-horizon, multi-turn bioinformatics benchmark for AI agents. Each of its 97 tasks links dependent subtasks into a directed acyclic graph, for 662 underlying subtasks in all. Each subtask is posed as one user turn and graded deterministically against an answer key, with optional in-context feedback on incorrect answers. Across 12 models on three harnesses, the slowest agent averages 3.6 hours per task, and no combination comes near saturating the benchmark, with mean scores of 0.43 to 0.57 out of a possible 1 without in-context feedback and 0.54 to 0.67 with it. Multi-turn evaluation also uncovers behavior that single-shot prompts cannot, as agents score lower on subtasks that depend on earlier subtasks, and in-context feedback helps agents recover some of that loss. Because deterministic scoring gives no qualitative account of how an agent failed, we also introduce an unsupervised failure mode discovery pipeline that describes, embeds, and clusters agents' failed attempts to surface emergent failure modes. Applied to OmicsBench, this pipeline exposes qualitative shortcomings in how agents carry out bioinformatics analyses, ranging from agents applying their own statistical filters in place of the specified ones to agents writing whole answers from memory.

2026/10/15 17:19 GTM

Step-level guards decide, before each tool call, whether an agent's next action is safe given the user's request and the trajectory so far. Users write those requests in many languages, while guard prompts and the benchmarks on which purpose-built guards are trained and evaluated are English. We ask whether a guard's verdict depends on the language of the request. We build LinguaGuardBench from the 1,220 labelled steps of TS-Bench AgentDojo-Traj: we translate only the user instruction into German, French, Turkish, Swahili and Amharic, and keep every tool call, observation and label byte-identical, so any change in a verdict is attributable to language alone. Six English rewordings and a whitespace placebo serve as controls, and managed guards are run three times to measure their noise. Across 19 guards, from frontier APIs to 4B open models and four purpose-built agent guards, most open, small and purpose-built guards change verdicts under translation beyond their own noise floor, and the effect grows with the rarity of the language: among the nine guards below 13B parameters, the mean placebo-adjusted flip rate rises from +1.5 pp on French to +4.4 pp on Amharic. Purpose-built guards are the most sensitive. The direction of the damage tends to follow guard size: open guards of 12-30B parameters block more benign steps (up to +5.0 pp false positives), and every significant loss of recall is in a guard below 10B (up to -7.4 pp). Frontier guards shift by at most about one point; the strongest, Gemini 3.8 Flash, returns identical verdicts with and without the request. The damage is concentrated in a few instructions and, in a post-hoc analysis, falls on steps where the agent's reasoning restates the English request, consistent with guards reacting to inconsistency between channels. English paraphrase moves every guard less than translation does. We release the benchmark and all 489k verdicts.

Supervised contrastive learning uses same-group positives and negatives from other groups, optionally including other tasks; heterogeneous structured batches accommodate tasks with different group counts. At inference, the model maps each object's image, text, and instruction directly to an embedding; HDBSCAN then forms a partition without the ground-truth cluster count. We construct ProductMIC from product metadata in Amazon Reviews 2023, comprising training data and an evaluation benchmark for instruction-conditioned clustering. The benchmark contains 5,373 products and 449 single-attribute and compositional grouping tasks spanning 50 leaf categories excluded from our training. With full-dimensional embeddings and HDBSCAN, direct within-task SupCon training achieves a mean per-task V-measure of 95.01, compared with 53.06 for WeMM 9B, the strongest general-purpose embedding baseline evaluated. Frozen product-trained encoders also improve out-of-domain conditional grouping, while controlled transfer experiments distinguish partition transfer from instruction response. We will release the code and dataset upon acceptance.

Effective medical simulators necessitate realistic haptic rendering of biological tissues that display viscoelastic material properties, such as creep and stress relaxation. Fractional-order models provide an effective means of describing intrinsically time-dependent viscoelastic dynamics with few parameters, as these models can naturally capture memory effects. However, due to the unintuitive frequency-dependent coupling between the order of the fractional element and the other parameters, determining appropriate parameters for fractional-order models that yield high perceived realism remains a significant challenge. In this study, we propose a systematic means of determining the parameters of fractional-order viscoelastic models that optimizes the perceived realism of haptic rendering across general populations. First, we demonstrate that the parameters of fractional-order models can be effectively optimized through active learning, via qualitative feedback-based human-in-the-loop~(HiL) optimizations, to ensure consistently high realism ratings for each individual. Second, we propose a rigorous method to combine HiL optimization results to form an aggregate perceptual map trained on the entire dataset and demonstrate the selection of population-level optimal parameters from this representation that are broadly perceived as realistic across general populations. Finally, we provide evidence of the effectiveness of the generalized fractional-order viscoelastic model parameters by characterizing their perceived realism through human-subject experiments. Overall, generalized fractional-order viscoelastic models established through the proposed HiL optimization and aggregation approach possess the potential to significantly improve the sim-to-real transition performance of medical training simulators.

We further introduce Cross-Image Margin Ranking (CIMR), which compares the same positive--negative caption pair across matched and mismatched images, encouraging a larger ranking margin when the image supports the correct description. ### Defining and Generating Compositional Structure
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20922) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.20922) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20922) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.20922)

2026/10/16 09:22 GTM

Compositional structure is pervasive in nature and across scales: atoms combine into molecules, molecules into cells, and cells into organisms, while further compositional hierarchies appear within each level. More broadly, compositionality, the reuse and recombination of parts into novel structures, is a fundamental source of interesting complexity in both natural and artificial systems. Yet despite its importance, and despite our apparent ease in recognizing it, mathematical accounts capturing what we intuitively mean by compositional structure remain limited. Here, we fill this gap by formalizing a simple idea: compositional structure emerges when parts, considered together, reveal shared structure that is not captured when those parts are modeled separately. We express this idea using algorithmic information theory, the mathematics of information and compression, which provides a natural way to identify structure in arbitrary data without specifying in advance what that structure must look like. Applied across scales, our definition informs where and how compositional structure emerges within an object. Using tractable approximations, we then invert the framework to generate compositional objects: we specify the desired form of their compositional structure without prescribing their particular content, and optimize directly over strings and images. The resulting objects exhibit readily recognizable hierarchical reuse and part-whole organization, suggesting that the definition captures compositional structure rather than merely complexity, regularity, or shared information. Finally, we discuss implications for machine learning, including a new perspective on depth in neural architectures and principles for constructing compositional data curricula capable of supporting open-ended learning.

Many collective-action systems are neither fully decentralized nor fully centralized: agents voluntarily organize into institutions that share organizational costs and delegate decisions to representatives. Delegation can reduce individual costs, but it also transfers control to representatives whose private objectives need not align with those of their constituents. We study this tension in a repeated game with endogenous delegation to self-interested centers. A simple reduced-form model yields a scale--accountability tradeoff: larger constituencies offer greater cost-sharing benefits to members, yet the marginal value to a center of retaining any one constituent decreases as group size grows. Consequently, the prospect of individual exit can sustain non-exploitative delegation only up to an endogenous accountable constituency size. Additionally, we identify parameter regions in which fully delegated, accountable governance, when feasible, must be polycentric. We investigate these predictions through simulations with large language model (LLM) agents. Across five matched pairs of 30-agent simulations, polycentric structures repeatedly emerge but remain fluid, exhibiting cycles of concentration, exploitation, exit, and re-formation. We further find that models of different capacity exhibit similar willingness to assume leadership but differ substantially in their exercise of authority, with Sol initiating exploitation more frequently than Luna in matched decision states.

### ACEdit: Stable Multi-Turn Image Editing via Asymmetric Conditioning
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22328v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.22328v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.22328v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.22328v1)

2026/10/15 17:52 GTM

Image editing models produce impressive results for individual edits, but their quality often deteriorates over multiple editing turns. Each editing turn introduces small distribution shifts that accumulate over successive edits, creating a gap between the input distributions encountered during the editor's original training and multi-turn inference. We introduce a training scheme that makes multi-turn image editing stable under this accumulating distribution shift. A key is to force the editor to operate on its own outputs from previous editing turns during training, bringing the principle of Self-Forcing to image editing. The main challenge is that self-generated editing trajectories deviate from ground-truth trajectories, making direct supervision unreliable. We address this challenge through a distillation objective that leverages a teacher image editor to provide a reliable training signal when the model operates on its own previous outputs. We introduce an asymmetric conditioning scheme: while the student performs edits sequentially on its own outputs, the teacher remains anchored to the original clean image and receives a compound editing instruction. Experiments show substantially reduced degradation over long multi-turn sequences while maintaining editing quality and instruction adherence.

Machine learning (ML) for analog IC design is constrained by limited open data and the absence of standardized evaluation: results reported in different papers are typically obtained on bespoke, often closed, datasets and with ad-hoc metrics, leaving comparisons across methods unreliable. Thus, the data presented here constitutes the most comprehensive dataset for ML development in over four decades of analog IC design automation. The dataset, ground-truth annotations, and reference implementations of the metric-defining routines are released under an open license, enabling reproducible and directly comparable evaluation across heterogeneous analog design automation methods.

The benchmark contains 2,400 rendered images built from 100 English Qur'an verses, four factual states per verse, and six visual conditions ranging from plain text to decorative and authority-framed layouts. All quote-reference pairs were source-checked and human-audited before rendering. Across 24 full model-prompt runs, we observe a sharp capability split. Proprietary API-served VLMs range from near-perfect citation verification to moderate false acceptance, whereas recent open-weight VLMs often show weak discrimination and default to VALID on most images. De-biased prompting can reduce false acceptance, but often by shifting the decision threshold toward INVALID rather than by producing strong citation discrimination. Visual Sanad therefore exposes a culturally grounded multimodal factuality failure: fluent VLMs may treat displayed religious citations as self-authenticating unless they have strong citation-verification ability.

Diffusion models are notoriously expensive to train, requiring large-scale datasets to learn how to gradually reverse the noise from images over multiple small denoising steps. Community question-answering platforms are important ecosystems for knowledge sharing, yet research on what makes an answer best or a question engaging has often focused on either individual lexical features or broad topics. This exploratory study examines both levels in Yahoo! Chiebukuro, asking which nouns and semantic noun clusters are associated with best-answer selection and question engagement. We analyze a large corpus of questions and answer-tier posts collected under the keyword logistics, using an offline machine-translation pipeline with manually validated noun-level fidelity. Engagement is modeled in two stages: whether a question receives any response and, conditional on receiving one, how many responses it attracts. Operational logistics nouns such as distribution, warehouse, and center are associated with best-answer selection, whereas broader civic and infrastructure nouns such as car, tax, and shinkansen are associated with higher engagement. At the cluster level, most semantic noun clusters are associated with best-answer selection, while the clusters associated with response likelihood and response volume are largely distinct. These findings reveal differentiated content-outcome profiles across answer and question levels and provide a domain-wide descriptive map of engagement and answer selection in logistics-related cQA discourse.

Existing federated data condensation methods face privacy risks, as uploaded synthetic data may preserve sensitive information and remain vulnerable to reconstruction attacks. Moreover, their generation often incurs considerable client-side computational overhead, limiting deployment on resource-constrained devices. To address these issues, we propose FedSumUp, which employs a learnable lossy compressor on clients to generate compact and visually interpretable representations. By controlling information capacity, FedSumUp reduces privacy leakage while allowing users to directly assess the privacy-utility tradeoff. We further introduce a dual-objective optimization mechanism to preserve task-relevant semantics while suppressing reconstruction risks. The compressor is trained on the server and only performs lightweight inference on clients. Since the server cannot access raw data, we develop a data-free closed-loop optimization strategy that optimizes the compressor by minimizing the discrepancy between original and twice-compressed representations after structure-preserving upsampling. Experiments on BloodMNIST, CIFAR-10, and ChestMNIST demonstrate reconstruction PSNR as low as 8.56 dB (with synthetic PSNR as low as 5.45 dB), client runtime reductions up to 96.5% (from 778s to 27s on ChestMNIST), and competitive classification accuracy (89.72% ChestMNIST, 82.24% BloodMNIST, 65.63% CIFAR-10), while remaining inherently robust to non-IID data distributions.

### ARC-SORT: Adaptive, Robust, Compensated Multi-Object Tracking with Interacting Multiple Models
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22306v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.22306v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.22306v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.22306v1)

2026/10/15 17:49 GTM

Multi-object tracking in sports broadcasts is hard for two reasons: athletes move fast and change direction abruptly, and the camera pans and zooms at the same time. We present ARC-SORT (Adaptive, Robust, Compensated SORT), a tracking-by-detection tracker that trains nothing and runs in real time. Its design rule: the tracker measures its own uncertainty, and that measurement decides how it acts. An Interacting Multiple Model (IMM) Kalman filter gives every track a predictive covariance, and we turn it into a per-track search region: the less reliable the motion prediction, the larger the region in which a detection may match (uncertainty-adaptive Expansion IoU, EIoU). On identical inputs, this continuous coupling is what matters: the same covariance used as a hard Mahalanobis gate hurts at every threshold we swept, and richer covariance summaries add nothing measurable. When two tracks cross, the assignment cost itself reports whether the decision was close. ARC-SORT keeps such pairs under review and applies at most one corrective identity swap once post-crossing appearance evidence shows the first decision was wrong; this adds +1.0 HOTA (higher order tracking accuracy) on both the SportsMOT and DanceTrack test sets at under a millisecond per frame. For players who leave the frame for seconds, motion extrapolation carries no information, so our offline re-identification drops the usual motion gate and links tracklets by appearance alone. ARC-SORT reaches 81.82 HOTA on the SportsMOT test set with the fewest identity switches of the 69 leaderboard entries as of August 2026 and tied-third multi-object tracking accuracy (MOTA, 97.09), and 62.1 HOTA on DanceTrack test with the same association settings. ### When Is Muon Optimal? Radial Fragility and Minimax Geometry
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21549) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.21549) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.21549) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.21549)

2026/10/16 09:22 GTM

Muon replaces a matrix gradient by an approximate polar factor and has shown strong empirical performance in neural-network training. The Isotropic Curvature Model (ICM) provides a curvature-based explanation for this update by asking when gradient orthogonalization minimizes a one-step local-loss surrogate. We investigate the stability of this explanation when the fixed input radius of the ICM is replaced by a general radial law. We introduce the Random-Radius Isotropic Curvature Model (RR-ICM) and the Polar-Factor Admissibility Set, which encodes exact scaled-polar optimality as a geometric condition on the positive polar factor of the gradient. We derive an exact characterization of this set for arbitrary radial laws and show that all nonscalar admissible responses arise from radial atoms aligned with curvature kinks. Consequently, every atomless radial law exhibits zero-capacity rigidity: exact scaled-polar optimality is possible only when the gradient is already a scaled column isometry. To connect the curvature-based framework with the established spectral geometry of Muon, we introduce the Minimax RR-ICM by replacing directional averaging with worst-case optimization over probability laws on the sphere. We prove that every nonzero global minimizer lies on the polar ray of the gradient, while its scale is determined by a one-dimensional convex problem. The corresponding admissibility geometry is a positive-semidefinite trace slab, in sharp contrast to the spherical-zonoid geometry of the RR-ICM. Together, these results expose the radial fragility of average-curvature explanations and provide a new minimax variational characterization of Muon's polar direction.

A perception system that must brake for a pedestrian does not need a low average latency. It must deliver an answer before the deadline on every frame, the worst case included. Commodity edge accelerators optimize the mean through dynamic scheduling, OS jitter, thermal throttling, and contention, so their per-frame latency can be measured but not bounded. We build FALCON, a camera-based 3D occupancy front-end on the AMD Kria KR260, an embedded FPGA drawing 4.3 W idle and up to 6.6 W under inference, and make the latency guarantee layered and explicit: programmable-logic (PL) compute is cycle-bounded analytically; inference is a measured envelope over 100k-frame traces; and a watchdog enforces output delivery, publishing the previous output flagged stale when a frame is late. The deadline runs use a fixed-work proxy of six ResNet-50 DPU inferences per frame, not the full occupancy network: across three 100k-frame enforced runs, all 300k proxy frames delivered an output within the 100 ms deadline plus at most 10.2 ms, the worst of the three runs. The full six-camera FlashOcc pipeline composes to about 1.3 s per frame on the board and is not yet a 10 Hz system. The two operations the DPU cannot run, a view-transform gather and a bilinear resize, become fixed-trip-count PL IPs numerically matched to their software references. On the full Occ3D-nuScenes validation set the board reaches mIoU 29.88, 92.8% of an FP32 baseline the DPU cannot run. Critically, single-head ablation produces $p{=}0.340$ and multi-head ablation of all three identified carriers produces $p{=}0.532$, meaning a causally verified annotation-reading circuit is completely invisible to output-level evaluation. ### TRACE: Topology-Aware Reconnection via Prediction-Adaptive Connectivity Enforcement for Thin-Structure Segmentation
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22210v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.22210v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.22210v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.22210v1)

2026/10/15 16:58 GTM

Thin, elongated structures are particularly sensitive to connectivity errors, as even a few local segmentation mistakes can disrupt otherwise continuous paths. Existing skeleton-based objectives typically supervise fixed centerline locations and do not account for alternative local routes that may restore connectivity. We propose TRACE, a topology-aware, prediction-adaptive objective that uses a target skeleton to define required local connections. For each connection, TRACE identifies the easiest valid reconnection under the current prediction and jointly supervises its bottleneck with the corresponding skeleton point. The supervision thus adapts to the evolving prediction rather than remaining fixed to the centerline. We further introduce a topology-aware skeleton carrier that preserves both foreground and class-specific topology, enabling reliable definition of these connections. Extensive experiments across six 2D and 3D benchmarks show that TRACE improves connectivity and topology while maintaining strong segmentation accuracy with low training overhead. Code will be released upon acceptance.

For the trend branch, we design a Latent Graph Block, employing latent graph modeling to capture global topological dependencies and system-wide co-movements. Conversely, for the seasonal branch, we introduce a Gaussian Multi-scale Attention mechanism to mitigate attention distraction. By injecting learnable Gaussian priors, we impose structural constraints that compel the model to focus on multi-granular local contexts, effectively filtering out global noise and accurately capturing local evolutionary phases. Extensive experiments on multiple real-world benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of our proposed model.

Label-free evaluation methods, such as self-consistency filtering, majority-vote aggregation, and agreement-based reliability scoring, rely on an implicit assumption that consistency can serve as a proxy for correctness when ground-truth labels are unavailable or expensive to obtain. Using four financial QA datasets (multiple-choice and free-form), ten consistency dimensions, and two frontier model families, we measure the joint distribution of consistency and accuracy at the level of individual examples rather than in aggregate. This high-consistency/incorrect quadrant cannot be detected by any evaluation signal that is built on agreement alone, as the model appears maximally reliable while being wrong. We show that the phenomenon is robust to the choice of consistency threshold t (it persists from t=0.50 to t=1.00), is shared across model families, and is concentrated in specific intersections of perturbation dimension and domain. We map where this quadrant occurs, analyze its stability properties, and discuss the implications for evaluation pipelines that use agreement-based signals in place of human labels.

Money-mule networks move criminal funds through many small transfers, each of which looks ordinary on its own. What marks an account as a mule lies outside any single transfer: in the counterparties and addresses its network shares, and in the shape of its activity over time. Production systems remain largely rule-based and see neither, while most research classifies transactions rather than accounts, recovering account-level risk only by aggregating afterwards. We instead score the account directly, reading the bank's relational schema as a heterogeneous graph in which rows are nodes and foreign keys are edges. Each account is scored from a multi-hop neighbourhood using per-column encoders, a relative-time encoding and HeteroGraphSAGE message passing, with that neighbourhood restricted to rows predating the account's own pre-flag cutoff so that no model sees evidence recorded only after the account was flagged. Our detector recovers nine in ten held-out mules at 83\% precision and a 0.5\% false-alarm rate, finding 115 more mules than the strongest tree ensemble given the same evidence. At the tuned thresholds the gap is mostly in recall: XGBoost and LightGBM lead across the first hundred alerts, but only the graph models hold their precision deep into the queue. Since the graph is the schema itself and every score is dated per account, the approach deploys on a bank's existing database without a custom feature graph build.

In this work, we introduce a motion- and gravity-aware framework under the Learning Using Privileged Information (LUPI) paradigm.   We integrate these priors into transformer backbones with lightweight architectural changes, preserving feed-forward efficiency. Experiments on standard benchmarks show consistent gains in reconstruction accuracy and robustness, especially in scenes with dynamic objects, ambiguous structure, and challenging camera motion. Our analysis further shows that the optimal injection stage is backbone-dependent: middle-layer integration is generally preferable for VGGT, while late-stage integration is more effective for π3. These results suggest that LUPI offers a practical way to inject motion and physical priors into feed-forward 3D reconstruction without increasing test-time complexity.

Understanding human communication is a long-standing goal in psychological research. Measurement and annotation of these signals has traditionally required time-consuming manual work by trained human labellers. The Nonverbal Interpersonal Communication Exploration (NICE) Toolbox is a free, open-source Python framework that automatically measures these signals by combining existing state-of-the-art computer vision and speech-processing methods into a single software package. It includes support for multi-camera systems, interchangeable detection algorithms, 2D and 3D visualization of detector outputs, evaluation on the user's custom data, and export to third-party software. With the release of this tool, we aim to make data exploration of interpersonal communication signals scalable, reproducible, and accessible for psychological research.

### 3DCodeVerse: Exploring 3D Worlds as Code
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22341v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.22341v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.22341v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.22341v1)

2026/10/15 17:56 GTM

We introduce 3DCodeVerse, a research ecosystem that enables coding agents to create editable and verifiable 3D content at scale. 3DCodeVerse comprises large-scale, high-quality datasets of executable 3D code, a coding harness for spatial reasoning and automated verification, and practical guidance for 3D coding. First, we construct 3DCodeVerse-2M, a large-scale curated dataset of standalone 3D executable code. We build this corpus by collecting open-source data, converting specialized representations, and generating new programs with coding agents. Each program is packaged with the source files and associated assets required for execution in its declared environment, along with available descriptions and verification records. Second, we develop the 3DCodeVerse Harness, a multi-agent directed acyclic graph framework for generating and refining 3D content. It uses execution checks, spatial measurements, and visual feedback to automatically evaluate and revise generated programs without human feedback during generation. Third, we conduct systematic empirical studies of 3D coding, establishing baselines with fine-tuned open models and deriving practical guidance through component-level analyses of our harness. Extensive experiments demonstrate that fine-tuning on our dataset significantly improves the 3D coding capabilities of open-source models. Notably, our 3DCodeVerse harness achieves a 90% win rate against Claude Design (3D Object) in pairwise human evaluations, when both are utilized by Claude Opus 5.5.

### A team of Gemini 4 Argon agents beats Minecraft
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22319) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.22319) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.22319) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.22319)

2026/10/16 09:22 GTM

We placed four Gemini 4 Argon agents in a sandboxed Minecraft survival world and gave them one goal: defeat the Ender Dragon. Each agent controlled a player via an open-source library called Mineflayer and coordinated with its teammates through in-game chat and shared memory. No one assigned roles or wrote game logic. The agents planned, wrote, and debugged every program themselves. Researchers could watch the run on a live dashboard and pause or stop it at any time, but they had no cause to intervene. Starting from a randomly generated world on Easy difficulty, the team wrote 2,783 programs and defeated the dragon in 27 hours and 14 minutes, dying 605 times along the way. To our knowledge, this is the first end-to-end autonomous completion of Minecraft by AI agents, achieved using only the standard Mineflayer library without pre-scouted seeds or other game-specific tools written by humans. Winning required the agents to keep a coherent plan across thousands of dependent steps for more than a day. Complex real-world software engineering and enterprise tasks demand the same long-horizon coding and multi-agent coordination. We describe the setup, how the run unfolded, the team's strategies (dividing the work, sharing what they learned, recovering each other's items), and the limits of the result.

We introduce Data World, a multi-agent engine that synthesizes environments, tasks, and rubrics from explicit mechanisms. Independent audits check target recoverability from observed files; rubrics support evaluation and RL rewards. Human preference-based curation for question realism selects 100 tasks across 50 held-out worlds and 16 domains for DataWorld-Bench. The engine also yields 2,135 training tasks from 1,037 disjoint worlds. RL-zero training on the synthesized tasks produces DataWorld-35B-A3B from Qwen3.6-35B-A3B without trajectory distillation. Under matched evaluation, it improves over its base by 7.06 points on DataWorld-Bench, 14.28 on DAComp, 20.90 on DABstep-Hard, and 12.13 on JobBench, a broader occupational benchmark.

[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21779v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.21779v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.21779v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.21779v1)

2026/10/15 12:36 GTM

Multi-modal semantic segmentation benefits from complementary sensors, but this benefit is fragile when some modalities are degraded or missing at test time. Many existing methods rely on dedicated fusion modules or multi-branch cross-modal encoders, and the fusion often becomes unreliable when modalities are absent, leading to substantial performance drops. We propose \textbf{FuseAdapt}, a parameter-efficient framework that performs fusion within the adaptation space of a frozen vision foundation model (VFM), exploring whether VFM priors can serve as an effective shared backbone for multi-modal segmentation. Instead of attaching a standalone fusion network, FuseAdapt assigns each modality a lightweight adaptation branch and performs \emph{adaptation-space fusion} by keeping Q/V low-rank adaptation private to each modality and routing only FFN low-rank residual activations across modalities inside the frozen backbone. The pretrained VFM remains fixed while modality-specific corrections and explicit cross-modal interaction are learned through the adaptation paths. To handle missing modalities, we further introduce \textbf{Predictive Modality Modeling (PMM)}, a direct representation-level objective for cross-modal redundancy. During training, PMM masks a target modality and predicts its embedding from the visible ones, supervised by a teacher target computed under full-modality input. PMM is used only during training and adds no inference-time overhead. Across three multi-modal segmentation benchmarks (MCubeS, DELIVER, MUSES), FuseAdapt consistently improves both modality-complete and modality-incomplete segmentation with a small trainable budget, yielding up to +3.01 mIoU under full modalities and +12.56 mIoU under missing modalities over prior state-of-the-art. Project page: https://devinxzhang.github.io/FuseAdapt-Page/.

Longitudinal electronic health records (EHRs) contain complementary diagnostic and laboratory information about patient health. However, irregularity, sparsity, heterogeneity, and incompleteness in documentation complicate EHR modeling. To address the problem, we introduce GraphBEAT (Graph-based Bucketized Event Aggregation over Time), a temporal graph representation learning framework that groups clinical events into time buckets and transforms observed features into patient-specific, temporally directed graphs to enable early risk stratification for gastrointestinal cancers. Each node in an EHR graph encodes feature identity, observed value, and temporal position of clinical events. A context-aware, time-decay message-passing mechanism models transitions between successive buckets, followed by multi-head attention pooling to generate patient-level representations. GraphBEAT was trained and evaluated on multi-site longitudinal diagnosis codes and laboratory tests to predict pancreatic, liver, and intestinal cancer at 12-, 24-, and 36-month diagnostic lead times. In all experimental setups, GraphBEAT consistently outperformed baseline graph neural networks (GraphSAGE, GAT, and GCN) and sequence modeling approaches (Transformer and long short-term memory [LSTM]). GraphBEAT also required over 250x fewer floating-point operations per patient and over 20x fewer parameters than the Transformer and LSTM baselines. These results support GraphBEAT as an accurate and computationally efficient approach for cancer risk stratification from sparse, heterogeneous longitudinal health records.

Recent feed-forward 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) methods have enabled effective multi-view 3D reconstruction by predicting Gaussian primitives in a single forward pass. However, existing approaches predominantly adopt a pixel-aligned paradigm that predicts one Gaussian per input pixel, coupling the Gaussian count to the input resolution and number of views. This also leads to redundant primitives in textureless regions and insufficient coverage in geometrically complex areas. We present Anchor3DGS, a pose-free feed-forward 3DGS framework that replaces pixel-aligned prediction with a compact anchor-based representation. Guided by information entropy, our method places a set of 3D anchors in the scene, concentrating them in regions of higher visual complexity while maintaining broad spatial coverage. Each anchor performs occlusion-aware aggregation of multi-view image features, exchanges information with other anchors, and is decoded into a set of local Gaussians. A lightweight feature enhancement module further refines the coarse RGB output from Gaussian splatting and improves rendering fidelity. Extensive experiments on diverse benchmark datasets demonstrate that our Anchor3DGS achieves state-of-the-art novel view synthesis quality while only using an order of magnitude fewer Gaussian primitives.

Consistency within a document is a crucial feature indicative of its quality. Recently, within the vast amount of information produced across various media, there exists a significant number of low-quality documents that either lack internal consistency or contain content utterly unrelated to their headlines. Such low-quality documents induce fatigue in readers and undermine the credibility of the media source that provided them. Consequently, research to automatically detect these low-quality documents based on natural language processing is imperative. In this study, we introduce a hierarchical graph convolutional network (HGCN) that can detect internal inconsistencies within a document and incongruences between the title and body. Moreover, we constructed the Inconsistency Dataset, leveraging published news data and its meta-data, to train our model to detect document inconsistencies. Experimental results demonstrated that the HGCN achieved superior performance with an accuracy of 91.20% on our constructedInconsistency Dataset, outperforming other comparative models. Finally, an ablation study further confirmed the significant impact of meta-data utilization on performance enhancement. We anticipate that our model can be universally applied to detect and filter low-quality documents in the real world.

Contrast-enhanced magnetic resonance imaging (CE-MRI) is crucial for accurately diagnosing and segmenting brain tumors. However, the acquisition of CE-MRI requires injecting contrast agents, which poses potential health risks and increases scan costs. Here, we propose a CE-MRI synthesis method from non-contrast sequences, reducing both risk and cost. Unlike existing methods that rely on channel-wise concatenation to fuse multi-sequence information for synthesis, we introduce a Mixture of Feature Difference (MoFD) module. The proposed module uses a dynamic weighting strategy to adaptively select and aggregate informative features from both individual source sequences and explicit differences among them. Quantitative and qualitative evaluations demonstrate that our proposed method outperforms baselines in terms of image fidelity and perceptual quality on the UCSF-PDGM and UPenn-GBM datasets. Our method successfully reconstructs tumor regions and achieves segmentation performance comparable to that obtained with real CE-MRI. Our ablation studies show that although adversarial learning improves perceptual quality, a regression-based variant of our method achieves higher structural fidelity.

Single image 3D generation aims to dress a given 3D mesh with relightable materials while faithfully preserving the visual details of a reference image. Existing methods either introduce artifacts when fusing inconsistent multi-view predictions or struggle to preserve fine details through implicit image-feature conditioning. To resolve these challenges, we introduce SUPRA-Tex, built on the principle of decoupling visible-material estimation, surface alignment, and observation-guided 3D completion. We then project these maps onto the visible surface and encode them with the pretrained material VAE encoder into native observation latents that share the generation target's latent space. An interleaved flow transformer holds these latents as fixed, read-only anchors while alternating unobserved-region and full-surface updates to generate complete material latents. This design achieves super-alignment with the reference, delivering What You See Is What You Get fidelity on visible regions and coherent completion on unobserved surfaces. Trained solely on public datasets, SUPRA-Tex achieves SOTA performance in both geometry-conditioned texturing and end-to-end asset generation, matching or even exceeding leading commercial systems on in-the-wild images. Project page: https://supra-3d.github.io/supratex/

[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21616v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.21616v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.21616v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.21616v1)

Medical segmentation uncertainty is often evaluated with calibration error or pixel-level error-detection scores, but these metrics do not show whether uncertainty improves downstream review decisions. We propose a decision-aware evaluation protocol that separates uncertainty estimation from deferral policy and evaluates their combination under fixed review budgets: which pixels, regions, or images should be accepted, and which should be reviewed. The protocol reports fixed-budget error reduction, risk-coverage behaviour, region-level review proxies, structural failure modes, probability-derived baselines, and sensitivity to review assumptions. We instantiate it across retinal vessel, skin lesion, and polyp segmentation with multiple backbones and uncertainty estimators. Under matched inference budgets and canonical multi-layer MC Dropout, test-time augmentation (TTA) gives a stronger error-detection signal across matched MC--TTA comparisons, including architecture and dropout-placement checks. However, estimator rankings are not universal. For example, probability-derived scores remain competitive, deep ensembles can outperform TTA, learned deferral helps mainly when the base estimator is weak, and rankings can change with review unit, uncertainty target, correction assumptions, and cost model. Structural and second-observer analyses further show that boundary and thin-structure failures are partly tied to annotation ambiguity. These findings support evaluating uncertainty as an estimator-policy-review configuration, not as a standalone score map. Code: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/Uncertainity-in-Medical-Image-Segmentation/README.md

### DINO-FIL: Multi-Layer Transformer Fusion for Generative Inpainting Forgery Localization
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21780v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.21780v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.21780v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.21780v1)

2026/10/15 12:36 GTM

We propose DINO-FIL, a transformer-based framework for pixel-level localization of generative image forgeries. The method leverages multi-layer representations from a pretrained DINOv3 ViT-H+/16 encoder combined with a lightweight fusion decoder to capture both semantic structure and fine-grained texture inconsistencies introduced by image editing. By aggregating intermediate transformer features across depths, the model improves sensitivity to subtle, spatially localized artifacts that are often missed by standard single-layer approaches. We evaluate DINO-FIL in a cross-dataset setting, training on B-Free and testing on Beyond the Brush, CocoGlide, TGIF, and SAGI-D. The model achieves strong generalization, outperforming existing baselines on average and on four of five evaluated datasets, while maintaining a high true negative rate on pristine images.

### Dense4D: Pixel-Level 4D Human Correspondence
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22325v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.22325v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.22325v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.22325v1)

2026/10/15 17:52 GTM

Reconstructing human motion in 4D requires identifying the same surface points through articulation, deformation and changes in visibility. We introduce Dense4D, to our knowledge the first feed-forward model dedicated to dense 4D correspondence across the body, clothing and hair from a set of monocular RGB images, without a body template. Trained specifically for human geometry, Dense4D jointly processes person-centred crops with known image-to-crop transforms to predict Human Point Maps (HPMs) and depth for each image. An HPM assigns each person pixel the 3D position of its observed surface point at a common reference time. These maps support amodal correspondence: points hidden in the reference image can be localised from observations in other frames. Matching HPM coordinates across crops establishes correspondence without predicting camera poses or large scene displacements; per-image depth then lifts the matches into 3D tracks. Experiments on real people demonstrate substantially higher tracking accuracy and closer agreement with scanned surfaces than the evaluated baselines. Points from different frames assemble into coherent human geometry in the reference pose, providing visible evidence of consistent dense correspondence through articulation and clothing deformation. Additional qualitative results, videos, and an interactive viewer are available at https://alpguler.com/dense4d.

Internal solitary wave (ISW) remote sensing requires task parameters and spatial information to be transferred across image acquisition, detection, stripe extraction, and propagation prediction. Here, we present ISW-Agent, which integrates large language model (LLM) interpretation with n8n workflows and independent scientific models through structured task constraints, geospatial transformations, and stage-specific output validation. In tests of 120 Chinese instructions per backend, GPT-5.6 Sol and DeepSeek both achieved a strict task-match rate of 94.2%, with all intents and execution decisions matching the reference. Sentinel-1 cases demonstrated continuous processing from dialogue requests to wavefront prediction for the specified scene. Of 50 timed scenes, 39 completed detection, extraction, and prediction with a median turnaround time of 48.04 s; 11 stopped after empty extraction. Across 3,408 controlled fault-injection runs, retries recovered 234 transient-failure conditions and output validation rejected 234 structural defects, while 61 provenance or completeness errors indicated the need for content-consistency checks. Overall, ISW-Agent thus connects natural-language requests to traceable spatial processing chains for multimodel ISW analysis.

Surveillance cameras along urban roads produce large volumes of unlabeled video that could support the monitoring of public transport, but grouping the observed vehicles into meaningful categories without manual annotation remains challenging, since buses from different routes often share the same body structure and differ mainly in their color schemes. This study presents an unsupervised clustering methodology for public transport vehicles that combines morphological features extracted with a frozen DINOv2 Vision Transformer and handcrafted HSV color histograms through weighted early fusion, followed by UMAP projection and K-Means clustering. The method was evaluated on 7,972 bus images extracted from about 1,460 hours of video of an arterial road in Mexico City, with the goal of isolating the buses of the city's public bus network (RTP) without supervision. With a fusion of 65\% morphological and 35\% color features, the pipeline produced 14 clusters that separate distinct routes and vehicle types, and it increased the precision of the target cluster with respect to morphological features alone. These results show that color complements self-supervised features for route-level clustering, provided that it does not dominate the representation.

### The Right Byte First: How UTF-16 Helps Multilingual Boundary Prediction
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22071v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.22071v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.22071v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.22071v1)

2026/10/15 15:31 GTM

Many hierarchical byte-level language models group bytes into patches using a learned boundary predictor whose view of the next codepoint depends on encoding and lookahead. In UTF-8, codepoints of many scripts take three bytes: 8 of 24 bits mark length and continuation, and the first byte carries only 4 high codepoint bits. After the same left context, a mark extending a grapheme cluster and a letter starting the next can thus share their first byte. Universal Byte-Level Encoding (UBE) keeps one- and two-byte UTF-8 encodings but writes these codepoints as two UTF-16 bytes, low byte first, so its first symbol carries 8 low codepoint bits that can distinguish such continuations. We train small causal predictors on matched FineWeb-2 text and evaluate on FLORES-200 under UTF-8, UBE, and UBE's same-length big-endian variant. At one-symbol lookahead, balanced grapheme-boundary error at codepoint boundaries across 17 languages in 13 Brahmic scripts is 8.76% with UTF-8, 8.62% with the big-endian variant, and 0.06% with UBE. When the target instead retains a parent tokenizer's subword cuts at grapheme boundaries, UBE lowers the mean error relative to UTF-8 at the same lookahead by 72.91% in Korean, 35.29% in Chinese, and 67.32-99.55% in eight more scripts. In the seven of them whose main Unicode block fits on one 256-codepoint page, the big-endian variant stays within 3.0% of UTF-8, so the gain follows byte order, not length. In Korean and Chinese, UBE's mean error falls below UTF-8's final mean error after only 25% and 37.5% of training. Which byte comes first thus affects how much lookahead a byte-level boundary predictor needs.

State-of-the art approaches to retinal vessel segmentation typically require extensive annotations, a highly time-consuming process, reliant on clinical expertise. We present FPG-Seg, an annotation-free pipeline for retinal vessel segmentation combining classical vesselness filtering, Bayesian deep learning and topology-aware graph reconstruction to segment retinal vessel topology without manual labels. Tested across four widely used benchmark datasets for retinal vessel segmentation, FPG-Seg attains a clDice of $0.74{-}0.78$, representing $86{-}98\%$ of the performance of an equivalent fully-supervised baseline, despite using no manual annotations for training or hyperparameter tuning. As part of this work, we also provide, to our knowledge, the first performance benchmark of an annotation-free vessel segmentation method on FIVES.

### VesperMUSE: Multi-Modal Fusion Vision Transformer for Image Semantic Segmentation Under Diverse Light Conditions
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21990v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.21990v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.21990v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.21990v1)

2026/10/15 14:39 GTM

Semantic segmentation is critical for the reliable navigation and scene understanding of autonomous systems, yet its performance degrades severely in low-light environments due to noise, artifacts, colour distortion, and the loss of structural details. To address this challenge, this work introduces VesperMUSE, a unified multi-modal framework designed to achieve robust image semantic segmentation across diverse illumination conditions. The architecture addresses the light deficiency problem on multiple levels: proposing a novel physics-informed synthetic low-light adaptation algorithm, approaching Low-Light Image Enhancement (LLIE) through a thermal-guided paradigm, and finally aiming to recover the intrinsic scene details for semantic segmentation through a multi-modal fusion approach. Following enhancement, the framework dynamically integrates the primary RGB features with auxiliary thermal representations and surface normal maps highlighting geometric discontinuities and material boundaries. At the core of this integration is a highly scalable, modality-agnostic self-attention mechanism that extracts both global context and fine local features from an arbitrary number of auxiliary inputs. By combining and projecting these hierarchical multi-modal representations into a unified component, the attention module allows the network to selectively attend to relevant cross-modal cues without requiring any architectural modifications when different sensor configurations are used. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that VesperMUSE successfully recovers missing visual cues, providing a flexible, state-of-the-art (SOTA) solution for machine perception in extreme and unstructured real-world environments.

This paper presents the Digital Typhoon Dataset V3, a new version of the longest typhoon satellite image dataset spanning nearly 50 years, designed to benchmark machine learning models on long-term spatio-temporal data. The latest addition to Dataset V3 is multispectral satellite data, enabling multi-channel models. We investigate data-splitting strategies and present benchmarking results for the Northern and Southern Hemispheres. Experimental results show that, under the current dataset construction and experimental setting, multi-channel inputs do not clearly outperform single-channel inputs for pressure regression. Among the evaluated model architectures, image types, and spectral channels, ResNet-18, full-resolution images, and Channel 1 generally provide the best single-channel performance for pressure regression. Performance also differs between the WP and AU datasets by regression target, with lower RMSE on WP for pressure regression and on AU for wind regression. These results highlight the importance of considering dataset-specific and task-specific characteristics when interpreting benchmark performance. Additionally, we analyzed systematic bias in intensity regression, which leads to underestimation of intensity for strong typhoons. We tested imbalance-aware weighting strategies, including inverse-density weighting inspired by BalancedMSE, and showed that these approaches are not sufficient to remove the bias. The dataset is publicly available at http://agora.ex.nii.ac.jp/digital-typhoon/dataset/ and https://github.com/kitamoto-lab/digital-typhoon/.

### Bridging the Markerless Tracking Validation Gap: Intraoperative X-ray Workflow Integration
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21696v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.21696v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.21696v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.21696v1)

Markerless intraoperative tracking of spinal anatomy via RGB-D sensors is a promising alternative to invasive bone-anchored fiducials, yet no existing clinical dataset provides independent subsurface ground truth for validating these methods. We address this gap with a clinically viable pipeline that derives per-vertebra 6-DoF pose from routine intraoperative C-arm fluoroscopy, co-registered to the RGB-D camera frame through a multimodal fiducial array detectable in both imaging modalities. Critically, the pipeline requires no additional radiation, instrumentation, or deviation from standard surgical workflows, relying solely on the fluoroscopic images already acquired during routine level checks. A calibrated transform chain links the vertebral pose recovered via multi-view 2D-3D CT-to-fluoroscopy registration to the RGB-D coordinate frame, enabling direct comparison of any markerless registration estimate against a subsurface reference. Ex vivo validation against optically tracked ovine vertebrae confirms end-to-end accuracy of 2.06 +- 0.29 mm and 3.35 +- 0.93 degrees, sufficient to serve as independent ground truth for methods with substantially larger errors. We demonstrate the pipeline's utility by evaluating two markerless registration strategies on clinical data spanning 11 vertebrae across 1,500 frames from three surgical sequences: the first such evaluation against an independent subsurface reference in a clinical setting. The proposed framework provides reusable infrastructure for benchmarking emerging markerless tracking methods on real surgical data.

[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21719v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.21719v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.21719v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.21719v1)

Arrested retinal development can cause childhood visual impairment, but grading optical coherence tomography scans is difficult to standardise across devices and centres. We developed and evaluated an unsupervised domain adaptation model to classify normal and abnormal retinal development across different OCT systems. This retrospective multicentre study used OCT scans from five centres in four countries and the UK Biobank. Data covered four OCT devices, including table mounted and handheld systems. A ResNet50 based Domain Adversarial Neural Network was trained using labelled images from a source device and unlabelled images from a target device. We evaluated six source to target pairings and compared model performance with seven clinician graders. A separate Heidelberg Spectralis dataset was reserved for external evaluation. Across validation sets, accuracy ranged from 87.75% to 96.06%, sensitivity from 88.68% to 98.38%, and specificity from 78.00% to 98.02%. The best performing model, adapted from TM OCT1 to HH OCT1, achieved 96.06% accuracy and an area under the receiver operating characteristic curve of 0.994, with a 95% confidence interval from 0.981 to 0.999. Several models also maintained discrimination on the unseen Heidelberg device. Unsupervised domain adaptation enabled OCT classification across devices without labelled target device data. The approach may support more consistent assessment of retinal development and large scale analysis of existing imaging datasets.
